#5202. Bitcoin crashes below $76,000 as AI bubble fears wipe $2.5 trillion from Wall Street

A violent selloff in technology stocks spilled into crypto, triggering $1 billion in liquidations as investors questioned the sustainability of the AI boom.

Technology stocks plunged after the Democratic midterm sweep intensified concerns over AI spending, dragging Bitcoin lower as leveraged traders suffered heavy losses.

Bitcoin hit an intraday low of $75,568 on Thursday and recovered to $76,830.67 as of press time, as a historic selloff in US technology stocks spilled into crypto markets and triggered more than $1 billion in liquidations across leveraged trading positions.

According to CoinGlass data, approximately $1 billion in leveraged positions had been liquidated over the previous 24 hours, affecting more than 201,000 traders.

Bullish positions accounted for $926.8 million, or nearly 89% of the total, compared with $118.2 million in bearish bets.

Bitcoin accounted for $264.2 million in liquidations, followed by Ethereum at $165.3 million and Solana at $33.5 million. The largest single liquidation was a $10.94 million ETH long position on Binance.

The heatmap includes equity-linked labels such as SOXL, SNDK and XYZ:MU, not only cryptocurrencies. The losses coincided with a sharp reversal on Wall Street, where the Nasdaq Composite plunged 4.9% and entered correction territory, falling more than 10% below its October record. The S&P 500 declined 3.2%, while the Dow Jones Industrial Average shed more than 1,100 points.

The Kobeissi Letter estimated that approximately $2.5 trillion had been wiped from US stock market valuations and reported that the Cboe Volatility Index, Wall Street's closely watched fear gauge, had surged more than 70%.

The market commentary firm attributed the speed of the collapse to excessive leverage meeting an abrupt change in investor sentiment, rather than a sudden deterioration in corporate fundamentals.

The selloff followed the Nov. 4 rally, when investors initially welcomed the Democrats' sweeping midterm victories as a potential constraint on President Donald Trump's economic policies.

However, attention quickly shifted toward the implications for the artificial intelligence industry, whose enormous capital expenditure commitments have helped sustain US equity valuations.

Investors are increasingly concerned that tighter political oversight, rising construction costs, and local opposition to data centers could undermine the expected growth in AI infrastructure spending.

“Such a ban, if even possible to enforce, would effectively mean the US surrendering its position in the global AI race to China.”

Those concerns have gained traction following Tuesday's elections. According to Ballotpedia, 46 of the 47 local data-center-related measures decided across the US in 2026 favored tighter restrictions, although some votes were advisory rather than legally binding.

The political backlash threatens to complicate an investment cycle built on expectations of continued demand for AI chips, electricity, computing infrastructure and data-center construction.

Nvidia fell below $200 for the first time since August, while the Nasdaq 100 declined approximately 5.5%, putting it on course for its steepest daily loss since April 2025.

Economist Mohamed El-Erian questioned whether the rest of the US economy could maintain its momentum if technology investment slowed.

He also cautioned against assuming that Democratic control of Congress would necessarily translate into a substantial slowdown in the technology sector.

Meanwhile, Citrini Research argued that expectations for AI infrastructure spending may have peaked as investors reassess whether projected returns can justify the industry's enormous capital requirements.

The research firm clarified that it was identifying a potential peak in spending estimates.

The distinction is significant for Bitcoin, which has increasingly traded alongside technology stocks during periods of financial market stress.

Thursday's simultaneous collapse in technology shares and cryptocurrency prices suggests investors are reducing exposure to speculative assets as uncertainty over AI investment returns, financing costs, and future regulation intensifies.

The next test comes as markets assess whether the political backlash will materially slow data-center construction or merely reduce expectations for how quickly the AI industry can expand.

#5201. Canton Coin Gets SEC Nod as Wall Street Tokenization Heats Up

Canton Coin Gets SEC Nod as Wall Street Tokenization Heats Up

The blockchain behind Wall Street’s newest tokenization service just got a regulatory nod for its native token.

The SEC’s Division of Corporation Finance said in a letter dated Tuesday that it views Canton Coin, the native token of the Canton Network, as a “digital commodity” under the agency’s March interpretation of how securities laws apply to crypto.

Agency staff also said they won’t recommend enforcement action if Canton’s validators and application providers mint the token programmatically without registering it.

The relief comes a week after the Depository Trust & Clearing Corp. launched a tokenization service that runs on Canton and DTCC’s own private network, letting firms convert DTC-held stocks and Treasuries into tokens. Morgan Stanley said Tuesday that it was among the first banks to use the service.

SEC staff pointed to two representations from Canton’s lawyers. The first is that the token derives its value from the network’s programmatic operation and from supply and demand, rather than from the managerial efforts of others.

The second is that the network has no “central party.” Canton Coin launched without an initial coin offering or premine, and tokens were initially earned only by running network infrastructure and applications, according to a statement from Canton. Canton’s request letter was dated Oct. 30, four days before staff responded.

The letter has limits, though, as it reflects only staff’s position on enforcement, not a legal conclusion, and rests on the facts Canton presented, the SEC noted.

“Financial institutions need confidence in both the technology they use and the economic model that supports it,” said Yuval Rooz, CEO of Digital Asset, which created the Canton Network.

Canton said the letter is the first relief of its kind since the SEC published its March interpretive release, which found that most crypto assets aren’t securities. It’s also the first since the agency’s August Regulation Crypto Assets proposal.

The decision fits a broader shift since the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate in September, with the SEC and CFTC taking the lead on crypto rules. SEC Chair Paul Atkins said in July the agency was ready to act if Congress didn’t, and the SEC recently proposed rules on how advisers and funds hold crypto.

Digital Asset raised $355 million earlier this year from Wall Street firms and a sovereign wealth fund.

#5200. Bitcoin (BTC) Predicted to Hit $86,000 This November: Kalshi

Bitcoin is finally regaining momentum after it retested previous low levels around the $75,000 mark following the recent crypto market volatility.

While market sentiment appears to be shifting following the brief price resurgence seen in the last day after Bitcoin reclaimed $80,000, traders on the Kalshi prediction market are regaining confidence in the asset and are heavily betting on further price increases this month.

Latest data showcased on Kalshi shows that the majority of traders on the platform have bet on Bitcoin reclaiming $86,000 before the end of November.

While $86,000 may seem like a pretty decent target, the price level was last reached on October 4, suggesting that Bitcoin has continued to trade below this mark for over one month.

After multiple weeks of sitting in the red, Bitcoin has suddenly flipped bullish, showing signs of a potential rally as it is now trading around $79,370 following a price increase of 1.79% over the last 24 hours.

At its current price level, Bitcoin would need to surge by about 8% within the next 25 days to reach the $86,000 target set by traders on Kalshi.

Bitcoin's historical data has drawn attention from market analysts as it aligns with the bullish predictions seen on Kalshi.

Over the years, Bitcoin has continued to record strong gains in November, making the month one of the most anticipated among traders as it ranks among Bitcoin's strongest performing months with an average return of 38.6% per CryptoRank data.

While this is not a guarantee of a potential price recovery for Bitcoin this month, it has further raised the hopes of traders for potential gains after the prolonged market downturn.

#5199. Analyst Floats $13 As XRP’s ‘Most-Likely Region’

Home » Ripple (XRP) » Analyst Floats $13 As XRP’s ‘Most-Likely Region’

Long-time crypto strategist explains the macro expansion influence over XRP’s long-term market value.

Egrag, the popular French crypto chart analyst, is floating around an idea of a double-digit XRP price tag during the next bull-cycle. The theory’s based on a macro expansion thesis visuals highlight specific price zones, including  $0.95, then $1.80 to $2 as early bullish confirmation.

He believes the weekly XRP chart presents a bullish trajectory that could develop over several years. From 2018 onward, the shared graphic projects strong macro bullishness via Elliott Wave-style counts combined with measured moves with targets up to $27 by the early 2030s.

Further, the veteran trader outlined the roughly $12 to $13 as “the most possible region”, basing this XRP prediction on the Elliott Wave theoretical rules. Abiding by those, Egrag established that the upcoming Wave 3 is normally 161.8% of the initial wave within the same structure.

The analyst has also drawn attention to the strategy used to buy & sell XRP coins. He urged his followers to treat the investment no differently than buying cars, furniture or real estate: things that require a consistent strategy & capital protection rather than capture every upswing.

#5198. South Korea wants to delay its 22% crypto tax again and give investors a new break on losses

The government now backs a 2029 start and a three-year loss carryforward, while the existing January 2027 timetable remains in place pending legislation.

South Korea’s government said on Nov. 5 it would pursue a two-year postponement of crypto income taxation to 2029, alongside a proposed three-year loss carryforward, in consultation with the National Assembly.

The announcement follows the Democratic Party’s Nov. 4 request for another delay, less than two months before the scheduled January 2027 start.

The government’s plan still requires legislation. National Tax Service guidance continues to specify taxation of eligible crypto transfers and lending from Jan. 1, 2027.

Party leader Kim Min-seok said the party wanted the Digital Asset Basic Act to establish an overall framework before giving companies and investors an adjustment period, Yonhap reported. He also identified overseas transaction information, computer-system verification and taxpayer guidance as preparations that should accompany another postponement.

The government cited expected crypto transaction-information exchange with the United States from 2029 and changes anticipated under digital-asset legislation as reasons for seeking two more years of preparation. It also proposed allowing losses to be carried forward for three years, so they could offset later gains when calculating taxable income.

Kim’s Nov. 4 remarks reported by Yonhap specified no replacement date, although the agency said committee members had mainly discussed two years. The government’s subsequent announcement now identifies 2029 as its target.

A 2029 start already appears in a legislative proposal. An Aug. 28 release from Rep. Kim Sang-hoon describes his Income Tax Act amendment to move commencement from Jan. 1, 2027 to Jan. 1, 2029. That proposed date remains conditional on an enacted change to the timetable.

Qualitative status map using the current NTS schedule and the government’s November 5 proposal. Solid and dashed connectors distinguish the existing schedule from the change requiring legislation; lengths do not measure time or probability.

Status as of Nov 5, 2026. The proposed 2029 date is not enacted; the graphic does not forecast passage.

The Democratic Party previously agreed to a two-year moratorium in December 2024. The NTS says an amendment passed that month produced the January 2027 timetable now facing investors.

Under the NTS framework for residents, crypto income comes from transfers and lending after acquisition costs and associated expenses are deducted. An annual 2.5 million won basic deduction applies, followed by a 20% national income-tax rate. The calculation therefore concerns income after costs and the deduction, rather than the total value of an investor’s holdings.

The combined rate is 22% when local income tax is included, as separately described in Kim Sang-hoon’s parliamentary release.

For crypto held before Jan. 1, 2027, the NTS uses the higher of actual acquisition cost and the asset’s 2026 year-end market value as the starting cost basis. Residents net annual gains and losses and report the income in May of the following year.

The 2027 dates describe the current scheduled regime. Moving the start to 2029 and introducing the proposed loss relief will require legislative changes.

#5197. Tether CEO Reacts to Massive USDT Demand in Paraguay

Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino has responded to claims that the company's stablecoin, USDT, is being increasingly sought after in Paraguay following a significant banking crisis that cut off millions of people from their savings. Ardinoino has described this as "a flight to safety."

As noted in a recent X post by Bitcoin proponent Samson Mow, Tether's USDT has seen a significant rise in value relative to the Paraguayan guaraní amid the collapse of Ueno Bank, one of Paraguay's biggest digital lenders.

Ueno Bank, with an estimated 3 million customers (a significant percentage of adult Paraguayans), has been hit by a major financial crisis.

According to Mow, USDT has risen from 5,692 guaraníes to over 6,000 guaraníes, an increase of over 5%.

The surge in interest in the U.S. dollar-backed stablecoin USDT, Mow argues, is due to people trying to safeguard their savings outside of the traditional banking system.

USDT's exchange rate rose from about 5,692 guaraníes on Oct. 15 to over 6,000 on Nov. 4.

It is worth noting that even the traditional U.S. dollar rose against the guaraní over the same period.

The rising interest in USDT comes as Paraguay faces a major financial crisis over the collapse of Ueno Bank.

On Nov. 3, Paraguay's central bank announced the start of resolution proceedings for the financial institution after discovering that Ueno Bank's solvency ratio had dropped below half the minimum limit.

The move essentially shut down Ueno Bank, cutting off millions of customers' access to their savings and standard banking services for the time being.

The crisis has also cast doubt on public funds parked at Ueno Bank, including hundreds of millions of dollars from Paraguay's social security system.

The scandal has reportedly already led to the arrest of eight people connected to the bank as well as the resignation of central bank president Carlos Carvallo, as reported by Reuters.

Paraguay's central bank has been trying to reassure depositors, stressing that the situation pertains specifically to Ueno Bank and is not indicative of the collapse of the country's banking system.

Customers with frozen funds at Ueno Bank cannot necessarily convert their deposits to stablecoins until they are able to access their money again.

#5196. Stellar Adds $127M in Tokenized Funds, XLM Tests Floor

Home » Altcoins » Stellar Adds $127M in Tokenized Funds, XLM Tests Floor

A whirlpool of RWA-tied inflows didn’t stop XLM’s price from testing the lower-boundary of a familiar pattern.

Stellar’s network just pulled in roughly $126.5 million in tokenized fund assets in a single day, more than 2.6 times the combined total of the next nine fastest-growing chains. At the same time, XLM sits near $0.15 and is drawing fresh attention after years of relative quiet.

According to data cited from RWA.xyz, Stellar added about $126.5 million worth of tokenized fund assets in a 24-hour window. That figure alone exceeded the next nine networks combined.

The jump underscores Stellar’s ongoing role as a preferred venue for regulated, real-world asset products, including funds from issuers already active on the chain.

Stellar adds $127M in tokenised funds in a single day.What a way to start the week for @StellarOrg.According to The @RWAFoundation_, Stellar's blockchain added an outrageous $126.5M worth of tokenised fund assets in a single 24-hour period.This works out to some 2.6x more… pic.twitter.com/LleFpWgAGP

While one day does not rewrite long-term rankings, the scale of the inflow shows capital is still choosing Stellar for certain tokenization use cases even as broader market conditions remain soft.

On the weekly chart, Stellar Lumen’s (XLM) has spent extended stretches doing very little before sudden moves that force late buyers to chase. Price is currently trading around $0.15.

$XLM has a strange relationship with patience.It spends years doing absolutely nothing, then suddenly moves like everyone needs to buy it at once.And looking at this weekly chart, I wouldn't be surprised if history repeats itself.$0.15 right now.$0.35 is the first real… pic.twitter.com/cMU62u9sAE

The first meaningful test sits near $0.35, with further levels of interest at $0.64 and $0.79. A clean break through those zones would open a path toward $1.30 as a longer-term scenario.

Regardless, none of those levels matter without sustained buying interest, but the historical Stellar rhythm of long dormancy followed by sharp expansion remains visible on the chart.

Strong network activity in tokenized funds does not automatically translate into immediate demand for XLM itself. Fund assets can grow on Stellar while the native token continues to trade in a tight range, especially when broader crypto markets are under pressure.

With the latest $127 million inflow, that puts Stellar’s XLM at a whopping $3.51 billion market cap in terms of RWAs. Right now, the network is capturing real capital while the token continues to test whether its long periods of patience are once again setting up a sharper move.

#5195. US Voters Back AI Data Center Restrictions in At Least 29 Communities

US Voters Back AI Data Center Restrictions in At Least 29 Communities

Voters in at least 29 U.S. jurisdictions backed bans or restrictions on data-center development Tuesday, according to The Washington Post, dealing a new setback to the massive infrastructure buildout behind artificial intelligence.

The measures ranged from outright prohibitions to requirements for additional public approval. Some were nonbinding advisory votes, meaning their passage does not automatically stop construction.

Ballotpedia tracked 41 data-center measures on November 3 ballots across 10 states. Twenty-eight were framed so that a yes vote would curb development, while 13 would permit or support projects, meaning rejecting one of the latter could also count as a defeat for developers.

Ohio produced some of the clearest results. All 10 charter measures backed by the grassroots group Conserve Ohio passed, according to the Post, and more than three-quarters of voters in Hubbard, Ohio, supported a ban on data centers.

The negative sentiment is so big that voters in Granville, Trenton, and Sunbury backed restrictions with margins of roughly 78% to 82%.

In Illinois, nearly 80% of voters in Joliet Township rejected an advisory proposal supporting AI data centers. In Janesville, Wisconsin, more than 60% approved a requirement for another public vote before major development at a former General Motors site linked to a proposed data center.

These facilities house the chips and servers used to train and run AI systems. Their appetite for electricity has turned proposed campuses into local fights over utility bills and grid capacity, while opponents also raise concerns about water use, noise and deals struck without public scrutiny.

AI companies are aware of the growing backlash and are already working to counter it. Some are stepping up lobbying efforts, while others are turning to public relations campaigns to reshape public perception. OpenAI, for instance, has sought to rebrand data centers as “compute campuses,” in an effort to distance the facilities from the negative connotations increasingly associated with them.

The votes arrived alongside victories by politicians promising tougher oversight of data centers. Democrat Gina Hinojosa defeated Republican Gov. Greg Abbott in Texas after campaigning for a moratorium on new projects, while Ohio's governor-elect Amy Acton also supports a pause and stricter rules.

Industry leaders have warned that blanket restrictions would put U.S. AI investment at risk. BlackRock CEO Larry Fink said last month that a broad moratorium could have serious economic consequences. "If we have a moratorium of some sort in this country, we will be in a recession," he said.

#5194. Hyperliquid (HYPE) Whales Turn Stan Druckenmiller's Hype Into a $5 Million Exit

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

According to a fresh investigation by Santiment, retail investors, giving in to "fear of missing out" on Hyperliquid (HYPE) price rally on October 30, gave four large wallets a perfect opportunity to fully close their positions, bagging $5 million in profit.

Wall Street legend Stan Druckenmiller's first ever appearance on X (former Twitter) with a post about Hyperliquid and the tokenization of trading was supposed to be a declaration of a new era for the popular DeFi exchange. It turned out to be a "sell the news" signal for HYPE tokens' whales.

The best example of 'cashing out' math was educated by the address "0xa2ce...3126". What was a constant accumulation since the end of May, at the peak of the pump, in the $94–$95 range for HYPE token, became a methodical dump worth exactly $225,000 on the market every five minutes. All this was happening while the crowd bought up on the news. The result is a completely empty wallet and its owner locking in $2,395,168 gains.

This whale was not alone in his approach and as Santiment stresses, citing its own on-chain data, four top players opened short positions simultaneously and are now in profit. The only big buyer who believed in HYPE price growth is losing more than 11%.

The token's price chart against USD only confirms a total exodus of big buyers at the time of writing. The Oct. 30 pump candle left a huge upper wick with a trace of those very five-minute sales. The HYPE price has already slid more than 12% and is squeezed in the $81.79–$85 range. The local level of $84.150 has been broken from top to bottom, and now the token is slowly drifting to the next strong support at $78.020.

Traditional finance's 'big guns' like Druckenmiller undoubtedly remain the best marketing tool for crypto projects. The only difference is that for retail investors a billionaire’s tweet became a call to action, and for big capital an exit order.

The buyers’ rush created a perfect liquidity window for the whales — a chance to sell off huge volumes of coins without the price slipping down. What this makes of the story is that the technology and business of Hyperliquid turned out not to equal the price of decentralized exchange's native token.

#5193. France budget rejection sidelines stablecoin tax, wallet reporting

Three adopted amendments, including stablecoin taxation and self-custody reporting, fall away but could be reintroduced in the Senate.

The French National Assembly rejected the 2027 state budget’s revenue section late Wednesday, removing crypto-related amendments from the version of the bill sent to the Senate.

The Assembly rejected the revenue section by a vote of 458–0 at first reading, with two abstentions, according to the official voting record. The revenue section covers government income, such as taxes.

After the rejection, the government will send the Senate the original budget text, without the crypto-related amendments adopted by lawmakers.

The proposals could still be reintroduced in the Senate and would need to survive the remaining budget process to become law.

Faustine Fleuret, global head of public affairs at lending protocol Morpho, told Cointelegraph before the vote that rejection of the revenue section could “result in the rejection of the entire bill in the National Assembly, including provisions adopted by way of amendments.”

The Assembly adopted three crypto-related amendments before rejecting the revenue section.

On Oct. 16, lawmakers adopted an amendment that would make gains from crypto-to-stablecoin conversions taxable.

On Tuesday, lawmakers adopted another amendment that would require taxpayers to disclose self-custodied crypto holdings totaling at least 100,000 euros ($112,000), with the threshold assessed on Dec. 31 of the tax year.

Another amendment would increase penalties from 10,000 euros to 50,000 euros for crypto platforms failing to provide information requested by tax authorities.

The Senate is scheduled to begin debating the finance bill on Nov. 20, followed by an examination of the revenue section’s articles on Nov. 23, according to its schedule.

#5192. Bitcoin traders face Friday’s $2.7 billion options expiry with a dangerous five-hour blind spot

Friday's contracts expire before the US jobs report, while hedging through the announcement costs about 74% more at the $80,000 put strike.

Bitcoin options worth approximately $2.7 billion expire Friday, five and a half hours before the US jobs report.

Data from Deribit shows 33,441 BTC in outstanding options contracts scheduled to expire at 08:00 UTC on Nov. 6, with the calculated maximum pain price sitting at $79,000.

The level is approximately 1.6% below Bitcoin's $80,280 current price, placing the expiry near the cryptocurrency's current trading range as investors prepare for another potential shift in US interest-rate expectations.

The contracts are heavily skewed toward bullish call options, which account for approximately 25,012 BTC in open interest, compared with 8,429 BTC in puts. That puts the put-to-call ratio at 0.34, with nearly three calls outstanding for every put.

The largest concentrations of call open interest sit at the $82,000 and $85,000 strike prices, while significant put positions are clustered between $72,000 and $75,000.

Deribit BTC call and put open interest for the Nov. 6, 2026 expiry. Approximate values digitized from a user-supplied Nov. 5 screenshot and rounded to 100 BTC. Very small or unreadable bars are missing (null), not zero; omitted strike labels also had no sufficiently legible bars. The screenshot marks $79,000 as max pain, not a price forecast. Intrinsic value is omitted because it uses a separate USD axis. Historical strike counts could not be checked independently.

Source: Deribit, BTC options open interest (user-supplied screenshot; estimates, Nov. 5, 2026) · XOOMAR, Deribit-based Nov. 5 snapshot (max pain and expiry-wide open interest cross-check only)

The $79,000 maximum pain level represents the settlement price at which outstanding options would collectively generate the lowest intrinsic-value payout. Although traders monitor the figure ahead of expiration, it does not guarantee that Bitcoin will gravitate toward that price.

Likewise, the $2.7 billion figure represents the notional value of outstanding contracts rather than money expected to enter or leave the market. Actual settlement outcomes depend on Bitcoin's price and how traders manage their positions.

However, Friday's expiry adds another layer of complexity because of its timing relative to the next major US economic release.

The Bureau of Labor Statistics will release October's employment report at 08:30 a.m. Eastern, or 13:30 UTC on Nov. 6, five and a half hours after Deribit's options settle.

That creates a potential protection gap for traders relying on Friday-expiring put options to hedge against a sudden Bitcoin sell-off.

Under Deribit's settlement rules, the contracts expire using Bitcoin's average index price between 07:30 and 08:00 UTC. Consequently, any price reaction to the subsequent employment report falls outside their protection period.

Investors seeking coverage through the announcement would therefore need longer-dated contracts or alternative hedges.

Deribit's Nov. 5 order book illustrated the additional cost. An $80,000 Bitcoin put expiring Nov. 6 carried an asking premium of approximately $393 per BTC of exposure, compared with $682 for the equivalent strike expiring Nov. 7.

The latter contract was roughly 74% more expensive, although the difference also reflects its additional trading duration and other market-pricing factors.

Protection extending through the Nov. 10 inflation report was even costlier, with an $80,000 put expiring Nov. 13 quoted at approximately $1,445.

The timing comes as investors assess whether the US labor market is weakening enough to influence the Federal Reserve's next monetary policy decision.

Analysts at Bitunix said markets expect the economy to have added 65,000 jobs in October, with the unemployment rate unchanged at 4.2%.

The analysts argued that stable employment would give the Fed greater flexibility to assess inflation before adjusting interest rates, while a significant deterioration could increase expectations of a more accommodative policy stance.

A stronger-than-expected employment report could instead reinforce concerns about persistent inflation and restrictive monetary policy, potentially putting pressure on Bitcoin through higher Treasury yields and a stronger dollar.

The upcoming report follows Bitcoin's recovery above $80,000 after its recent decline, leaving traders to assess whether improving market sentiment can withstand another macroeconomic shock.

Friday's sequence will provide two separate tests. The 08:00 UTC settlement will resolve the outstanding options positions, while the employment report later that morning will determine whether traders need to reposition for a changed interest-rate outlook.

With October's inflation figures due Nov. 10, demand for longer-dated protection could remain sensitive to how sharply Friday's payrolls data changes market expectations.

#5190. Solana developers propose major overhaul to address hidden risk that could disrupt its network

Anza CEO Brennan Watt wants a common rulebook for validator software as Agave and Firedancer operate independently, potentially reducing dangerous disagreements over blockchain rules.

Solana developers are considering a new protocol rulebook designed to make independently developed validator software more reliable and easier to maintain.

The proposal, published Oct. 30, comes from Brennan Watt, CEO of Anza, the blockchain engineering company behind Agave, one of the main software programs used to operate the Solana network.

Watt wants Solana to establish a single, authoritative specification defining how its blockchain processes transactions, validates blocks and reaches consensus, rather than relying on rules scattered across existing software and separate technical proposals.

The change could address a growing challenge as Solana expands beyond its original validator software to include independently developed alternatives such as Firedancer, built by trading firm Jump Crypto.

Validators are computers that verify transactions and help secure the blockchain. Although different developers can create the software running these computers, every implementation must follow the same underlying rules to maintain agreement across the network.

Disagreements over those rules could cause validators to process identical transactions differently, potentially creating conflicting views of the blockchain and threatening network reliability.

Watt argues that Solana's existing development process makes it unnecessarily difficult for independent teams to determine exactly how the network is supposed to behave.

Under the current system, protocol changes are proposed through Solana Improvement Documents, known as SIMDs. These documents describe individual upgrades, but the network's complete operating rules are not consolidated into one authoritative reference.

As a result, developers sometimes have to examine existing validator code or reconstruct previous upgrade decisions to understand which rules are active.

Watt said this approach can produce repeated or inconsistent descriptions of existing behavior while making it harder to identify every part of the protocol affected by a proposed upgrade.

Under the proposal, developers would continue discussing potential upgrades through the existing SIMD process, but formal technical changes would be submitted as edits to a shared protocol specification.

Each amendment would describe precisely how the upgrade affects transactions, network communications, consensus and other relevant functions.

The changes would undergo technical review and any required governance approvals before becoming part of the official specification when activated on Solana's Mainnet.

This would allow teams developing Agave, Firedancer and future validator software to work from the same documented requirements rather than relying on another implementation's code as their primary reference.

Watt has already published a draft specification covering 18 areas of Solana's operations, including transaction processing, network communications, consensus, fees, and validator rewards.

However, the draft remains limited to a defined period of Solana's blockchain history and assumes developers begin from an already trusted network state. Further work would be needed to cover subsequent upgrades and establish that implementations remain compatible over time.

#5189. 70.5 Million POL Heist Triggers Polygon’s Hard Fork Proposal

Home » Altcoins » 70.5 Million POL Heist Triggers Polygon’s Hard Fork Proposal

A controversial governance proposal requires a hard fork to retrieve the looted balance in two separate incidents.

Two previous hacking incidents on Polygon’s Proof Of Stake (PoS) chain have looted nearly 70.50 million tokens, worth approximately $6.5 million at the current rates. A fresh hard-fork proposal from a member of the Polygon Labs Validator Support Team is looking to retrieve the sum via new amendments.

The idea is to move them from the attackers’ addresses into recovery wallets designated and verified by Binance & Polymarket, the platforms where the Polygon (POL) related accidents took place. On 29th and 30th October 2026, Polymarket and Binance each dealt with separate POL-tied hacking instances.

The block-producing POL nodes are presently restricting these funds from traveling across the blockchain. However, if this Polygon (POL) dev proposal gets granted, an extreme measure is expected to be taken: the frozen funds are sent back to a recovery address that Polymarket & Binance designated & verified.

#5187. US Bank Regulator Issues First AI Framework as Banks Battle Rogue Agents

US Bank Regulator Issues First AI Framework as Banks Battle Rogue Agents

The U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency issued its first supervisory framework for artificial intelligence in banking today, telling examiners to focus less on how powerful an AI system is and more on what happens when it makes a mistake.

The OCC's framework addresses that very concern, although the regulator has not linked its issuance to those specific incidents.

In a section covering operational resilience, the agency warns that external agents operated by customers, fintechs, or other businesses could create risks for banks even when the banks don't control those systems.

It suggests examiners consider measures including identity verification, delegated permissions, transaction limits, monitoring, and the ability to suspend or revoke access.

The guidelines aim to tackle the dilemma of how to let customers use AI assistants to manage their finances without giving autonomous software unrestricted access to accounts that banks are already facing.

The new OCC guidance arrives less than two weeks after reports that major banks were discovering unauthorized third-party AI agents operating inside customer accounts, sometimes taking actions that appeared to go beyond users' instructions.

Morgan Stanley was blocking outside agents, while JPMorgan, Bank of America, and Citi were restricting access, according to an Axios report on October 22. Bank executives described the situation as "horrifying," raising questions about who controls an autonomous agent once it has access to someone's money.

The OCC's framework makes a distinction between an AI system's technical sophistication and the damage it could cause.

A simple agent authorized to initiate financial transactions may present greater risk than a more advanced model limited to writing internal documents, the regulator said.

Under the 22-page framework, examiners can consider the consequences of failure, whether an action can be reversed, and how much control humans retain over the system.

The agency outlines four illustrative risk tiers. Fully autonomous payment agents and real-time credit decision systems fall into the highest category, while basic internal productivity tools occupy the lowest.

Higher-risk systems may warrant tighter restrictions on autonomy, independent testing, and human approval before consequential actions can proceed.

The framework also addresses AI systems that continue operating while making harmful decisions. Suggested safeguards include circuit breakers, rate limits, restricted permissions, and emergency shutdown controls.

Those considerations are especially relevant to the outside agents described in Axios's earlier reporting, where banks struggled to distinguish legitimate customer-authorized software from systems operating beyond their intended permissions.

The OCC stressed that the document does not create new regulatory requirements or enforceable standards for banks.

Instead, it gives examiners a way to evaluate AI within existing operational, compliance, and cybersecurity risk categories. Banks are not required to establish a separate AI risk category.

The document uses the mandatory term "Super Intelligence," which the Donald Trump administration insists on using in place of “artificial intelligence,” but its scope includes conventional AI and “autonomous agents” rather than only hypothetical systems with intelligence beyond human capabilities.

The framework covers AI used in payments, fraud detection, customer service, and other banking operations. It also extends its risk considerations to external AI agents connecting through bank channels.

The OCC said it intends to update the guidance as the technology develops. For now, its illustrative risk scale puts autonomous payment agents in the highest-risk category, regardless of how well they perform in testing.

#5186. XRP Ledger Welcomes New Euro Stablecoin

The XRP Ledger (XRPL) community has welcomed a new stablecoin to the blockchain. This time, it's via GateHub, which is launching a new euro-pegged stablecoin for blockchain payments that links the blockchain to the traditional European banking network.

As announced in a press release on Nov. 5, GateHub partnered with Slovenian financial services company Dinaro to launch GateHub Euro (EURGH), a MiCA-compliant (European Union's Markets in Crypto-Assets Regulation) stablecoin pegged to the euro.

The new stablecoin operates on both the XRP Ledger and Xahau, an alternative blockchain in the larger XRPL ecosystem with smart contract capabilities.

Part of the significance of EURGH is its SEPA (Single Euro Payments Area) integration. With this, GateHub users can make instant euro payments from their XRPL and Xahau wallets to banks in Europe.

GateHub notes that these transactions can take just seconds to complete, linking blockchain assets to the traditional banking world.

GateHub Euro is a stablecoin that is designed to maintain a value of exactly one euro. This means 1 EURGH should equal €1. It is an electronic money token (EMT) under the EU's MiCA regulations.

GateHub Euro is issued by Dinaro, which is an authorized electronic money institution regulated by the Bank of Slovenia.

According to Dinaro's published terms, it will issue EURGH on a 1:1 basis when it receives Euro funds. Holders of the token will be able to redeem EURGH for Euros at face value (subject to proper checks and compliance procedures).

GateHub users can access traditional European payment systems via their digital wallets because of the SEPA integration. This is an alternative to traditional crypto exchanges for converting funds into the traditional banking network.

GateHub Euro is not the first euro-pegged stablecoin to emerge in the crypto space or on the XRP Ledger.

For instance, there's the MiCA-compliant euro stablecoin EURØP from Schuman Financial.

Meanwhile, there's Circle's EURC, which is a euro-pegged stablecoin for payments and trading on multiple blockchains.

And there's EUR CoinVertible (EURCV) from Société Générale-FORGE, a digital asset business unit of French banking giant Société Générale.

#5185. South Korea seeks 4th crypto tax delay, targets 2029 start

South Korea is seeking a fourth delay to its 22% crypto tax, months after reaffirming plans to begin taxing digital asset gains in January 2027.

South Korea’s government has backed postponing tax on cryptocurrency gains by another two years to 2029, in what would be the fourth delay to its implementation.

Finance Minister Lee Hyoung-il announced the proposal Thursday, saying the government would consult lawmakers on postponing the January 2027 implementation date.

The tax would impose a 22% levy, comprising a 20% income tax and 2% local tax, on annual crypto gains exceeding 2.5 million won ($1,860).

The government cited 2029 as the start of information exchange with the US under the Crypto-Asset Reporting Framework (CARF). It said imposing the tax before those exchanges begin could put the domestic market at a disadvantage relative to overseas markets. It also wants to account for changes under planned digital asset legislation.

Officials also said they plan to introduce a three-year loss carryforward provision, allowing investors to offset crypto losses against profits earned over the following three years. The government plans to submit amendments to improve the tax system during parliament’s regular session in 2027, with detailed tax guidelines planned for the first half of 2028.

The tax was originally scheduled to take effect in January 2022 but was subsequently postponed to 2023, 2025 and 2027.

The proposal reverses the government’s previous position. A Finance Ministry official said in May that implementation would proceed in 2027.

South Korean finance officials recently engaged with major crypto exchanges amid growing calls to postpone the tax. Industry representatives cited unresolved tax rules for different transaction types and insufficient time to prepare the necessary infrastructure.

#5184. Treasury bought back $6B in bonds, but dealers say bigger isn’t enough

Treasury's latest $6 billion buyback shows why Wall Street dealers want the government to rethink how it buys bonds, not just how much it buys.

The US Treasury bought back $6 billion of older government bonds on Nov. 4, hitting the full limit of its latest operation. On paper, that looks like a success for a program designed to make the Treasury market easier to trade.

But there's a catch. One bond accounted for $4.9 billion, or 82% of everything Treasury agreed to buy. Add another $950 million from a second bond, and just two securities made up almost 98% of the entire purchase. Of the 40 bonds eligible for the operation, only seven were bought.

This is the kind of concentration Wall Street dealers have been worried about for months. In feedback published by Treasury on Nov. 4, dealers said the larger buybacks were helping market liquidity.

However, they also explained why simply increasing the program size might not be enough to achieve that.

Their feedback came before Wednesday's operation, so they weren't responding to this particular result. We now know the Treasury can buy billions of dollars of bonds and still leave dealers holding the securities they hoped to sell. The amount purchased is important, but so is how those purchases are spread around.

The Treasury market is enormous, but not every government bond trades equally easily. The newest issues attract the most attention and typically have the deepest markets. Older bonds, known as off-the-run Treasuries, can be harder to sell quickly without accepting a lower price.

That's where dealers come in. They buy bonds from customers, keep them on their books, and look for someone else willing to take them.

But holding those bonds costs money, sometimes a lot of money. That's why dealers often have to borrow to finance their positions, exposing them to price changes while they wait for another buyer. The longer they hold a bond, the more those costs and risks compound.

Treasury's liquidity-support buybacks give them another potential buyer: the government itself.

In September, Treasury began larger buybacks of longer-dated bonds, at least doubling the previous $2 billion maximum for those operations. The idea was to provide a more reliable outlet for older securities.

According to the Nov. 4 dealer feedback, most dealers reported at least modest improvements. They said the purchases had made it easier to find an exit for older bonds, reduced the risk of sudden price distortions, and helped keep pricing relationships between similar securities more stable.

While that's meaningful progress, the Treasury got mixed answers when it asked whether the changes had improved dealers' ability to handle customer trades.

A lack of unity among dealers is what makes these buybacks so controversial. We now have dealers that welcome a large government buyer but aren't necessarily becoming more willing to buy bonds from customers.

If the Treasury keeps concentrating its purchases in a handful of issues, dealers holding other, older issues still have to find other buyers or keep financing the position. In other words, the buyback program can work without working equally well across the entire market.

Dealers wanted better, earlier communication about operations, smaller purchases more frequently, and limits that would help distribute Treasury buying across a wider range of individual securities.

More frequent operations would give dealers more opportunities to sell, as they'd get another chance sooner.

Limits on how much of one security the Treasury can buy could also prevent a single bond from absorbing most of the available capacity. Each bond has its own identifying number, called a CUSIP, so dealers suggested using per-CUSIP limits to spread purchases more broadly.

Some dealers specifically warned about the risk of missing a concentrated buyback and being left to manage the interest-rate exposure from bonds that remained on their books.

This is exactly what we saw on Wednesday. According to the official data, Treasury received $39.6 billion in offers for an operation capped at $6 billion. That means dealers and other eligible participants offered more than six times what Treasury ultimately accepted.

And despite that demand, one bond maturing in November 2041 absorbed more than 80% of the purchases.

Nov. 4, 2026 operation, accepted face value in USD. Labels identify coupon and maturity. All seven purchased securities are shown; 40 were eligible. Eligibility does not show which unpurchased issues were offered. Face value is not cash expenditure. The largest share is calculated as $4.924 billion / $6 billion. Settlement: Nov. 5.

Source: U.S. Treasury: Nov. 4 buyback result, pages 1–2

It's worth being careful about what those numbers tell us. Treasury doesn't publish the full breakdown of rejected offers, so we don't know whether every one of the 33 eligible bonds that received no purchases was actually offered. We also can't assume Treasury made a bad decision by concentrating its buying.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

This is because buybacks are extremely competitive operations. Treasury evaluates the prices offered and the relative value of the securities, so if one bond presents particularly attractive offers, buying more of it may make sense for taxpayers.

The problem is that getting the best available prices and helping the widest range of sellers aren't necessarily the same thing.

Nov. 4, 2026 operation, USD face value. Treasury accepted $6 billion against $39.636 billion offered, filling the $6 billion maximum. These overlapping measures are compared, not added. The offer ratio is 39.636 / 6 = 6.606, rounded to 6.61. Offered face value does not count distinct sellers or unique inventory. Filling the maximum is not a measured liquidity improvement.

Source: U.S. Treasury: Nov. 4 buyback result, operation totals

Treasury also has existing limits designed to preserve the amount of individual securities circulating in the market. Dealers are asking for changes that could distribute purchases more evenly within those constraints.

They discussed other possibilities, including alternative pricing methods and transactions that would exchange one security for another. But those would be more complicated to introduce, and Treasury said they would require further work.

For now, the most immediate requests are simpler: communicate earlier, buy more often, and avoid having so much of the operation go to just one security.

For investors watching Treasury buybacks as a potential source of liquidity for Bitcoin, a $6 billion buyback doesn't automatically mean $6 billion in fresh liquidity is headed toward risk assets.

First, the $6 billion is the face value of the debt Treasury agreed to retire. Because those bonds were trading below face value, the cash purchase amount was approximately $4 billion before accrued interest.

Second, Treasury continues issuing new debt to finance the government even as it buys back older securities.

So the program primarily changes which bonds are outstanding and how easily dealers can manage them. It doesn't automatically increase the amount of money available to buy Bitcoin.

However, a connection may still exist through financial conditions. An Oct. 30 Federal Reserve study, using data through June 2025, found that Treasury buybacks reduced dealers' bond inventories and eased some risk constraints for trading desks.

But the researchers found no statistically significant reduction in dealers' repo activity.

Repo is essentially short-term borrowing backed by securities. It's one way dealers and their customers finance bond positions. That's important because taking bonds off dealers' books doesn't necessarily eliminate the financing demands tied to the broader market.

For Bitcoin to benefit, easier Treasury trading would need to translate into better financing conditions or greater investor capacity to take risk. Wednesday's purchase result alone can't tell us whether that happened.

The next question is whether Treasury changes the program in ways that make those improvements more consistent.

In its latest quarterly plan, Treasury said it anticipates up to $70 billion in liquidity-support buybacks from November through January, alongside a separate $25 billion for cash management purposes. It also confirmed that it's evaluating changes to purchase limits, scheduling, frequency, eligibility, and execution.

That gives dealers a chance to see whether their feedback leads to something concrete.

The real market test won't be whether Treasury manages to spend its full buyback allowance. It'll be whether more bonds become easier to trade, dealers become more willing to handle customer orders, and financing pressures actually ease.

A $6 billion buyback might look impressive on paper, but for a dealer still holding a bond nobody wants to buy, the announcement's size only goes so far.

Armed with a classical education and an eye for news, Andjela dove head deep into the crypto industry in 2018 after spending years covering politics.

Also known as "Akiba," Liam Wright is a reporter, podcast producer, and Editor-in-Chief at CryptoSlate. He believes that decentralized technology has the potential to make…

#5182. Coinbase Says 99% of Customer Crypto Is Offline and Out of Hackers’ Reach

Coinbase Says 99% of Customer Crypto Is Offline and Out of Hackers’ Reach

Coinbase is moving to reassure customers that what happened at Binance can’t easily happen there.

In a blog post Wednesday, the U.S. exchange said it wasn’t affected by last week’s hack of Binance, in which attackers drained about $6.4 billion from the rival exchange’s hot wallets.

Coinbase said it keeps more than 99% of customer assets in cold storage, meaning offline wallets that can’t be reached over the internet. It moves funds into internet-connected hot wallets only as customers need them.

The Binance incident “has understandably left customers asking” how Coinbase guards against a similar attack, the post said.

Coinbase laid out several layers of protection. Private keys for its cold wallets are split into pieces and never fully reassembled, even when signing transactions, and no single employee has standing access to the hot-wallet signing service. Strict limits cap how fast funds can move from cold to hot wallets, and unusual withdrawal spikes are held until staff review them. The exchange can also trigger “kill switches” that take signing keys offline entirely.

The company said it assumes employees will be targeted by phishing, so its controls are designed so that a stolen password, session or device can’t be used on its own, and sensitive actions require multiple approvals.

That focus on insiders carries weight for Coinbase. In May 2025, it disclosed that criminals had bribed overseas customer support agents to steal personal data on less than 1% of its monthly transacting users, then demanded a $20 million ransom that Coinbase refused to pay.

Last week, Coinbase said a security incident at analytics vendor Amplitude exposed contact data for a “small subset” of customers, though funds weren’t affected.

Coinbase also said customer assets are held 1:1 and aren’t lent out unless customers direct it to, and it reported $8.6 billion in cash and cash equivalents and $377 billion in assets on its platform as of Sept. 30. It flagged artificial intelligence as a fast-changing threat and pledged further security investment.

The Binance theft, which the FBI has attributed to North Korea’s TraderTraitor group, has rattled the industry. Blockchain analytics firm Elliptic found that the laundering spanned about 12,000 wallets in under four days, while stablecoin issuers froze about 44% of the haul.

Separately, Brazil’s Mercado Bitcoin halted withdrawals after a $40 million hack, and French bank Crédit Mutuel Arkéa recently confirmed a cyberattack that led to a $133 million crypto theft.

#5181. Gate boasts 104% cold reserve coverage, but separate snapshot dates raise red flags

Its new hot-wallet loss test uses separate measurement bases, while independent verification of current PoSEC data remains in progress.

Crypto exchange Gate has introduced Proof of Security, a disclosure framework that proves its cold assets cover customer balances if it lost all its hot-wallet assets.

Its Nov. 5 announcement reports 104.58% cold-storage coverage, using a storage snapshot taken at 00:00 UTC on Nov. 4.

The published percentage compares asset shares from different snapshots, rather than establishing cold assets and customer balances on a single common date. Gate discloses that limitation, so customers need more than the headline ratio to verify the full-backing claim.

Gate's PoSEC methodology compares an 88.26% cold-storage share with an 84.39% user-asset share. The remaining shares are 11.74% in hot wallets and 15.61% owned by the platform.

Cold storage is measured against cold assets plus hot-wallet assets in the storage snapshot, while user assets are measured against platform reserves in the separate Proof of Reserves snapshot. The FAQ defines the coverage calculation as the first share divided by the second.

The page explicitly says the snapshots have different totals and that intervening price changes and fund movements are not reflected.

Same-date backing needs aligned balances. Gate’s reported percentage combines shares from different snapshot totals.

The reported 104.58% share comparison alone cannot establish backing at one common date.

Different snapshot dates and totals; intervening price changes and fund movements are excluded. The earlier PoR reference is not separately dated in the chart. This does not establish insolvency or losses.

Rings normalize each reported share to its own 100% total; they do not compare absolute dollar values. Arc lengths use the displayed percentages. 88.26+11.74=100 and 84.39+15.61=100. The 104.58% result is Gate-reported, not recomputed from rounded display inputs.

Different snapshot dates and totals; intervening price changes and fund movements are excluded. The earlier PoR reference is not separately dated in the chart. This does not establish insolvency or losses.

Gate's Oct. 21 disclosure reports roughly $8.7 billion in reserves and $7.3 billion in user net assets as of Oct. 18, with a 118.49% overall reserve ratio.

The PoSEC chart does not separately date its earlier user-asset reference, and the October dollar totals cannot be combined with November storage percentages to present a measured dollar buffer.

A direct test of backing after a hot-wallet loss would compare surviving cold assets with customer balances at the same time, using compatible valuations and the same reporting perimeter.

The disclosure leaves a gap in demonstrating common-date coverage, as it doesn't establish either insolvency or customer losses.

Gate's definition includes digital assets in cold wallets and traditional assets, such as stocks and US Treasuries, held by independent custodians.

The exchange says qualifying assets must sit outside routine deposit and withdrawal operations, be offline or independently custodied, require multiparty transfer authorization, and be verifiable. It also explains that off-chain traditional assets can make reported cold storage exceed balances visible in blockchain cold wallets.

As of Nov. 5, the PoSEC page lists independent third-party verification as in progress. Gate says the review covers asset authenticity and control, valuation and reporting scope, and the coverage calculation.

Gate's PoSEC FAQ says individual wallet addresses are currently withheld for security reasons, making the promised independent report particularly relevant to public scrutiny.

That review should be distinguished from the older audit shown on Gate's Proof of Reserves page. The page dates a Hacken implementation assessment to Jan. 3, 2024. Hacken describes that work as examining code, architecture, build processes, and vulnerabilities.

The new framework gives customers a concrete scenario to evaluate. The decisive next evidence is a common-date reconciliation of cold assets and customer balances, with valuation methods, entity scope, custody restrictions, and asset composition clear enough to assess the claim.

A completed review can show how those pieces fit together, while a comparison of percentages from different snapshots cannot do that by itself.

#5180. Google Tells Schools They Can Reject AI—But They'll Lose Search, Too

Google Tells Schools They Can Reject AI—But They'll Lose Search, Too

Google is giving schools more freedom to reject artificial intelligence—provided they're also willing to give up Google Search, which is arguably what everybody in the world uses Google for.

The company announced Tuesday that its AI products will be disabled by default for K-12 student accounts unless administrators explicitly enable them. But schools that want to ban AI entirely will also have to disable Google's search engine, a tool millions of students rely on for everyday research.

Chris Phillips, Google's vice president and general manager of education, tried to justify the reasoning during a meeting with educators.

“Search and AI are deeply integrated,” he said according to Education Week. Schools uncomfortable with AI can choose not to enable Search at all.

Google says administrators will be able to activate or disable Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom, and Search individually. That means schools can block the standalone chatbots while keeping Search available, but they cannot remove AI from Search itself.

For teachers who want students to research sources without receiving AI-generated answers, the company is effectively offering two options: accept its AI-powered search or find another search engine.

The announcement is already drawing accusations that Google is using its dominance in education to push a technology many schools are still trying to understand, regulate or restrict.

Google says the changes are intended to address concerns that AI chatbots can undermine learning by giving students ready-made answers instead of encouraging them to solve problems themselves.

Beginning in early 2027, schools that enable Search will be offered versions with conventional AI features, such as AI Overviews and AI Mode, or the education-focused Guided Learning experience, according to The New York Times.

Neither option provides the traditional search experience without generative AI.

New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani accused the company of “trying to use its monopoly power to force AI into our public schools,” according to CBS News.

Former Federal Trade Commission chair Lina Khan was equally critical, accusing Google of using its search monopoly to coerce schools into adopting AI.

The criticism comes as some of America's largest school districts are actively restricting generative AI. New York City, for example, imposed a one-year moratorium on student-facing generative AI through eighth grade, covering nearly 600,000 students, while Los Angeles has also restricted the technology.

Los Angeles officials had specifically hoped to preserve Google Search while removing AI-generated summaries. Under the newly announced controls, that won't be an option.

Google's position also arrives amid continuing antitrust scrutiny. A federal court ruled in 2024 that Google unlawfully monopolized the U.S. search market, and the company has faced separate legal challenges over its decision to integrate AI summaries into search results.

School administrators are expected to receive the new controls in early 2027. They will be able to decide which AI products students can access, and even apply different settings by grade level. A Google Search option without AI, however, is not among the choices.

That said, for schools that want to, ahem, unofficially ban AI without losing Google search, our guide may provide a quick solution. It takes 5 minutes and lasts forever.

#5179. Optimism new proposal weakens council power over standalone chains

The proposed policy would let qualifying transfers proceed without another governance vote, changing who users rely on for upgrades.

An Optimism governance proposal published Nov. 5 would require at least 30 days' public notice before a chain transfers upgrade control from the shared Security Council, giving users time to assess changes to the network's security model.

The proposed Standard Rollup Charter amendment concerns chains whose upgrade authority rests with the Council under the Charter. It would let a Chain Governor request that control move to an address of its choosing, so it could manage upgrades itself or through a third party.

A completed transfer would mean relying on the security model of the new upgrade key holder. Optimism Governance and the Security Council would no longer retain the same practical ability to enforce the Charter's policies on that chain through upgrade authority.

The policy's accompanying GitHub amendment PR 74 was still open on Nov. 5 and says it will merge only if governance accepts the proposal.

A governor would first submit a request to the Optimism Foundation, which would confirm that it is valid and complies with the governor's commitments. The Foundation would then publish notice on the Governance Forum.

That notice should identify the chain, its Chain ID, and material layer-1 contract addresses. It should also warn that the chain would cease to be a Standard Rollup if the transfer goes ahead.

The Security Council would have to wait at least 30 days from publication before executing the transaction. The proposed policy says the Council should check that the Foundation published the notice, that the minimum period has elapsed, and that the recipient address matches the one the Foundation supplied on the governor's behalf.

A transfer meeting the approved policy's conditions would need no additional Optimism Governance approval. That would remove the need to seek a separate vote for each handover.

#5178. Bitcoin win rate vs. S&P 500 recovers as post-midterm history favors stocks

Bitcoin outperformed the S&P 500 in half of the past 90 US trading sessions, while historical midterm data highlights gains for stocks.

Bitcoin (BTC) has outperformed the S&P 500 in half of US trading sessions over Glassnode’s latest measurement period, ending a prolonged stretch of losing more sessions than it won.

Data from onchain analytics platform Glassnode shows that Bitcoin outperformed the S&P 500 in 50% of Wall Street trading sessions over a rolling 90-session period.

The reading, covering the 90 sessions through Wednesday, marks an improvement after an extended period in which Bitcoin lost more sessions than it won.

“The win rate has not been above 50% since March 2025; for most of the past year, Bitcoin lost more sessions than it won,” Glassnode wrote in the latest edition of its regular newsletter, The Week Onchain.

BTC/USD vs. S&P 500 per-session returns. Source: Glassnode

Glassnode added that Bitcoin’s average per-session gains were also “slightly higher” than those of the S&P 500.

Democrats’ wins in the US midterm elections have also drawn attention to historical stock market gains following similar results.

Data from crypto asset manager Capriole Investments shows that Democratic sweeps of Congress under Republican presidents have historically been followed by strong S&P 500 returns.

Related: Bitcoin hits $80.5K as US stocks rally on midterm election results

Of the 26 midterm elections since 1922 (excluding this year’s vote), Democrats won both the House and Senate under a Republican president seven times. The S&P 500 gained an average of 10.7% over the following three months.

Average gains were 17.5% after six months and 15.5% after a year.

“There is simply no better election result for markets than we just had,” Capriole founder Charles Edwards commented on the data in a post on X.

S&P 500 performance based on Midterm Election outcome. Source: Charles Edwards on X.com

Data from TradingView puts Bitcoin’s rolling 20-day correlation with the S&P 500 at 0.63. The measure has become markedly positive since August, when both crypto and stocks gained as the US Treasury expanded its debt buybacks.

#5176. Unpatched Bitcoin Lightning nodes face channel-draining bug

Core Lightning urges Bitcoin node operators to patch payment and channel security flaws and tighten remote-access credentials.

Core Lightning, software for operating Bitcoin Lightning payment nodes, is urging users to install v26.06.10 to address channel-security, payment-resolution, and remote-access flaws.

Maintainers also recommend reviewing the credentials used for remote commands, making access restrictions a separate task alongside the software upgrade.

The release notice recommends upgrading as soon as practical. Maintainers have temporarily withheld security-fix tests to slow the conversion of patches into working exploits and give operators more time to upgrade.

The new version adds checks on a peer's transaction signatures during Bitcoin dual-funded channel opening and splicing, following the Lightning protocol's BOLT 2 specification, according to the tagged changelog.

A conditional Bitcoin payment contract, known as an HTLC, that had just been removed could be confused with a live one sharing its payment hash, amount and expiry. The node could then fail the live payment upstream even though its peer could still claim it.

Another fix rejects channel opens when a peer proposes a dust limit above 5,000 satoshis. The changelog says the previous behavior let a peer trim the node's balance into fees during a forced close. Dual-funded opens also reject a peer's dust limit when it exceeds the channel reserve.

Dual funding remains experimental, and maintainers discourage zero-confirmation channels with untrusted peers. The release also refuses a v2 zero-confirmation open unless the operator has configured acceptance for that peer.

Nodes that have run master cannot downgrade into the 26.06.x series because their database schema is newer.

The peer generally needs a rune, an authorization credential permitting the command.The update closes a way to bypass rune restrictions on parameter names using duplicate or punctuation-variant names. It also fixes a commando crash triggered by reusing a command ID after a failed command and rejects oversized runes that previously consumed excessive decoding work.Maintainers recommend binding runes used over commando to the application's node ID through a id restriction. They also advise operators to blacklist runes they cannot account for using blacklistrune, then issue replacements with tighter restrictions.

Core Lightning's commando interface lets a directly connected peer request that a node execute a command. The peer generally needs a rune, an authorization credential permitting the command.

The update closes a way to bypass rune restrictions on parameter names using duplicate or punctuation-variant names. It also fixes a commando crash triggered by reusing a command ID after a failed command and rejects oversized runes that previously consumed excessive decoding work.

Maintainers recommend binding runes used over commando to the application's node ID through a id restriction. They also advise operators to blacklist runes they cannot account for using blacklistrune, then issue replacements with tighter restrictions.

#5174. ChatGPT Is Getting a GIF Maker Powered by OpenAI's Unannounced Images 2.6 Model

ChatGPT Is Getting a GIF Maker Powered by OpenAI's Unannounced Images 2.6 Model

The internet may soon have an unlimited supply of AI-generated dancing cats, courtesy of ChatGPT.

OpenAI is rolling out a feature that lets users generate animated GIFs directly inside ChatGPT, according to The Verge. The feature reportedly comes powered by its new image-generation model called Images 2.6, which OpenAI has yet to formally introduce through its usual product announcements.

Users can describe an animation in plain English or upload an existing photo and ask ChatGPT to bring it to life. Once the GIF is generated, they can adjust its playback speed, inspect individual frames, make edits, and share the finished animation.

OpenAI demonstrated the capability with a simple request: “can you make a gif of a cat dancing?” The resulting interface reportedly includes controls to change animation speed and examine the GIF frame by frame.

We tried generating GIFs using ChatGPT, but the feature was not available on our accounts at the time of testing. In our interaction, the model generates a series of frames, but not the animation.

The rollout appears to be gradual, so users may have to keep asking for their dancing cats a little longer.

The GIF maker is reportedly part of a broader Images 2.6 update that promises faster image generation. The Verge reports that OpenAI is bringing the model to ChatGPT and Codex users across web, desktop, and mobile platforms.

Developers are also getting two new API models, GPT-Image-2.6 Flare and GPT-Image-2.6 Sunburst, designed for faster generation and higher-quality results, respectively. OpenAI has not yet published a standalone announcement detailing the new model's capabilities, and we could not independently test its performance.

The update follows the September release of ChatGPT Images 2.5, which introduced sharper details, more precise editing, and generation speeds up to 50% faster than its predecessor.

In our previous testing, Images 2.5 went head-to-head with Google's Nano Banana 2 across six categories, with each model winning three. OpenAI's model delivered particularly impressive illustration quality and improved on the oversharpening problems that affected earlier versions, although it still made mistakes when rendering text.

The move into GIF generation also comes months after OpenAI shut down Sora, its standalone AI video-generation app, in March. Animated GIFs are considerably simpler than the videos Sora generated, but the new feature brings a form of motion generation directly into ChatGPT without requiring a separate application.

OpenAI has not provided a specific date for when every ChatGPT account will receive GIF generation.

#5170. Thailand to block Bitget in December over unlicensed crypto trading

Thailand’s SEC filed a criminal complaint against Bitget’s operator, with access to its website and app set to be blocked from Dec. 5.

Thailand’s securities regulator has filed a criminal complaint against BG Limited, the operator of cryptocurrency exchange Bitget, for allegedly operating without a license.

Thailand’s Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) said Thursday that it had notified the Ministry of Digital Economy and Society to block access to Bitget from Dec. 5. The restriction will cover Bitget’s website, other associated domains and its mobile app.

The regulator advised users to take action regarding assets held on the platform before the deadline.

The complaint follows Bitget’s Oct. 20 announcement of plans to acquire a licensed Thai crypto exchange.

According to the SEC, Bitget offered crypto trading services between 2022 and 2025, charged trading fees and promoted its services in Thai through its websites and social media channels. The regulator said these activities violated Thailand’s digital asset business licensing requirements.

The complaint also names Kritsana Kritsananuwat, whom the SEC accused of helping promote Bitget through Facebook and YouTube. The watchdog previously filed a criminal complaint against him in March 2025 over allegations that he helped promote OKX’s unlicensed exchange activities.

Cointelegraph attempted to contact Kritsananuwat through a phone number listed for advertising inquiries in a video on his YouTube channel but was unable to reach him.

Thailand introduced licensing requirements for digital asset businesses in 2018 and tightened the rules in April 2025 to cover foreign platforms targeting Thai users. Changes to a separate cybercrime law also made it easier for the ministry to block access to unlicensed platforms.

Related: Thailand SEC says several companies eye crypto ETFs, none has applied

The regulator filed the complaint with Thailand’s Economic Crime Suppression Division for investigation. Prosecutors will decide whether to bring charges, while courts will determine criminal liability.

Bitget did not immediately respond to Cointelegraph’s request for comment.

Bitget announced plans on Oct. 20 to acquire GMO-Z.com Cryptonomics, a locally licensed crypto exchange and brokerage, through BTGT Management (Thailand), a company in which Bitget said it held an interest.

The proposed acquisition remained subject to regulatory approval and other conditions at the time of the disclosure. Bitget said it intended to introduce its trading platform to Thai users after completing the transaction and obtaining approval for the change of control.

“Thailand is an important part of Southeast Asia’s digital asset landscape, and this acquisition gives us a great opportunity to establish local operations,” Bitget CEO Gracy Chen said at the time.
