#5036. Why Am I Always So Tired? 15 Reasons You May Have Fatigue

Allergies, stress, and sugar could all play a part in your energy levels.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

The most relatable statement of all time: “I’m exhausted.” Life is stressful, busy, and often feels like an impossible balancing act. Beyond the demands of endless work tasks and social obligations, legit health reasons could explain lingering fatigue. If you’re chugging coffee on the regular and still wondering, Why am I so tired? it's worth considering whether something in your body is causing your low energy.

Obviously, if you stay up binging a new series or push yourself harder than usual while training for an upcoming race, you’re gonna feel sluggish the next day. But there’s a difference between feeling tapped out sometimes and long bouts of fatigue that won’t go away.

Meet the experts: Antoinette LaDawn Price, MD, MPH, is a family medicine doctor at Emory Physician Group Practice; Purvi Parikh, MD, is an allergist and immunologist with Allergy & Asthma Network; Sobia Khan, MD, is the director of Women's Center for Comprehensive Care at Baylor College of Medicine; Jessica Cording, RD, is the author of The Little Book of Game-Changers; Monifa Seawell, MD, is a board-certified psychiatrist in Atlanta, Georgia

“Fatigue is chronic extreme tiredness,” says Antoinette LaDawn Price, MD, MPH, at Emory Physician Group Practice. Fatigue can show up as headaches, dizziness, and achy and sore muscles, irritability, brain fog, and moodiness; it manifests differently in each person.

If you always feel drained, it may be time for you to consult your doctor about your symptoms. If you feel like something is off, don’t ignore it—there are many health issues, both mental and physical, that can make it difficult for you to last through the entire day without wanting to collapse in bed. Here are possible causes of fatigue, according to doctors.

Classic symptoms of seasonal allergies include sneezing and having watery eyes. Less common symptoms include low energy and fatigue. “If left untreated, seasonal allergies can cause fatigue,” says Purvi Parikh, MD, an allergist and immunologist with Allergy & Asthma Network. Why? Your body works hard to fend off the allergen, and that takes extra energy. That’s true whether you're allergic to outdoor triggers like ragweed or pollen, or to indoor allergens like dust mites, Dr. Parikh says.

Allergens aren’t the only things that can trigger fatigue—allergy medications can, too. “Some of the over-the-counter allergy pills like antihistamines unfortunately cause fatigue as well,” says Dr. Parikh.

How to fix it: If allergies leave you feeling completely spent, your current treatment plan may not be working. So, check in with your doctor about your options. They’ll likely recommend medication (or a different one) to help get things under control. Running a HEPA air purifier at home can also help clear the air, notes Dr. Parikh.

While going hard during your at-home workouts can wear you out, the opposite can do the same. “The human body gets more tired with progressive sedentary habits due to generalized reconditioning of the body and, ultimately, muscle loss,” explains Sobia Khan, MD, an assistant professor of general internal medicine and the director of Women's Center for Comprehensive Care at Baylor College of Medicine.

Basically, your body takes a “use it or lose it” approach to fitness. If you don’t exercise regularly, you’ll feel more tired when you start moving and even when you’re at rest.

How to fix it: This solution is pretty simple—get moving more. Just a reminder, the general physical activity recommendation is 150 minutes of moderate-intensity exercise each week, plus muscle-strengthening activity at least two days a week. Once you start moving again, it’ll be easier to get through your day-to-day stuff without being a zombie.

When you eat sugary foods or foods high in refined carbs (think: white bread, pasta, pastries), your blood sugar spikes, according to Jessica Cording, RD, author of The Little Book of Game-Changers. In the moment, that can make you feel kinda hyper. But unfortunately, you're also in for an inevitable crash, which can leave you feeling totally wiped.

If you keep overloading on sugar and refined carbs, you may also develop insulin resistance, Dr. Khan adds. That is when your body can’t use glucose (a.k.a. sugar) as well as it should for energy.

How to fix it: Cording says to cut back on sugar and refined carbs regularly. FWIW, the CDC recommends limiting added sugar to less than 10 percent of your total daily calories. (So, if you eat a 2,000-calorie diet, no more than 200 of those calories should come from added sugars.)

When you’re having sugary or carb-heavy foods, balance the meal with protein and fat, like adding nut butter to your crackers. This can help lower the odds that you’ll crash afterward, Cording says.

Stress isn’t just mental—it can affect you physically, too. “Your mind and body are closely linked and are in constant communication with one another,” says Monifa Seawell, MD, a board-certified psychiatrist in Atlanta, Georgia. “When there is a shift in your emotional state, be it positive or negative, it is not at all uncommon for that change to also show up in some physical way in your body.”

You can start getting headaches, an upset stomach, and, yep, fatigue, Dr. Seawell says.

How to fix it: The best approach is to address the root of your stress. While some stressors are unavoidable, doing your utmost to limit the ones you can address can be helpful.

Dr. Seawell also recommends scheduling 20 to 30 minutes of exercise each day. She says this can often boost your energy levels when you can’t completely eliminate your stress. If you’re still struggling, try talking to a licensed mental health professional. They “can help you process your emotions and learn healthy coping skills,” Dr. Seawell says.

Adults should be getting seven hours or more a night, according to the CDC. Definitely getting less? Enter: Fatigue.

“Minimum seven hours of sleep is required for effective rejuvenation of the body and restfulness of the brain,” says Dr. Khan. Here’s the thing: Even one night of bad sleep can mess with your circadian rhythm (your sleep-wake cycle) and throw you off for a few days, she says.

How to fix it: At baseline, the solution is simply to get more sleep. But sometimes that’s easier said than done. You need to first figure out what the culprit is—stress? Netflix? Crappy sleep hygiene?—and then address it. If that still doesn't work, talk to your doctor about your options. You may need an intervention such as cognitive behavioral therapy to treat insomnia, for example.

Depression can cause a range of symptoms, and decreased energy or fatigue is definitely one of them, according to the National Institute of Mental Health (NIMH). “Whenever I am screening a patient for depression, feelings of fatigue and loss of energy are one of the symptoms I always inquire about,” says Dr. Seawell.

These are the symptoms of depression to be familiar with, according to the NIMH.

Worth noting: Not everyone with depression experiences every symptom, per the NIMH. So, it’s entirely possible for you to feel tired on the regular and not even realize that it’s due to depression.

How to fix it: In this case, you need to address the underlying cause of your fatigue before you’ll feel better. Depression can be treated with psychotherapy, medication, or a combination of the two. If you suspect that your fatigue is linked to depression, though, it’s best to talk to your doctor about getting evaluated.

Your thyroid is a small, butterfly-shaped gland in the front of your neck that produces hormones that regulate how your body uses energy. When it doesn't produce enough thyroid hormone, it causes a condition known as hypothyroidism, which can mess with or slow down many of your body's functions, according to MedlinePlus.

“Thyroid hormones have receptors on essentially every organ of the human body and their deficiency can cause fatigue by excessively slowing the metabolic pathways,” explains Dr. Khan.

How to fix it: The treatment for hypothyroidism is taking medication to replace the hormone that your own thyroid can no longer make or create a sufficient amount of to keep your body functioning normally. Just know this: It may take a little while to feel better. Your doctor may need to adjust your medication until you reach the optimal level.

Just because you’re on the mend from, say, a virus doesn’t mean you should expect to bounce back energy-wise automatically. Recovery can take time. "A viral or bacterial infection can cause dehydration, generalized inflammation, and immense fatigue," says Dr. Khan.

How to fix it: First, give yourself time to recoup. Drink plenty of water and just take it easy, and you should feel less tired and more like yourself in a week or two.

Anemia is a condition in which your blood has fewer than normal red blood cells or hemoglobin, according to the National Heart, Lung, and Blood Institute (NHLBI). Hemoglobin is an iron-rich protein that helps red blood cells carry oxygen from the lungs to the rest of the body. According to the NHLBI, if you have anemia, your body doesn't get enough oxygen-rich blood. So, if you have the condition, you can struggle with “fatigue and excessive tiredness,” says Dr. Khan.

How to fix it: It depends on the type of anemia you have. For some types of mild to moderate anemia, you may just need to add OTC or prescription iron supplements to your regimen. But your doctor may also suggest certain vitamins, IV therapy, or medicines that make your body produce more red blood cells, according to the NHLBI. You may also need to incorporate more iron-rich foods into your diet. Basically, you should see your doc for this one.

Gluten is a protein found in wheat, barley, and rye. In some people, gluten damages the small intestine and causes symptoms like bloating, diarrhea, constipation, gas, nausea, stomach pain, vomiting, and yep, fatigue, according to the National Institute of Diabetes and Digestive and Kidney Diseases (NIDDK). That happens to people with celiac disease.

This condition can also cause long-term digestive issues that keep you from getting the nutrients you need and, in turn, cause ongoing fatigue.

How to fix it: Celiac disease can be tricky to diagnose, but after a physical exam, your doctor may recommend a blood test and an intestinal biopsy to check for inflammation in your small intestine. If you are confirmed to have celiac disease, you’ll need to avoid foods containing gluten in the future.

This is a less likely diagnosis, but it’s still worth mentioning. MS is an autoimmune disease in which the immune system attacks the brain and spinal cord, damaging the myelin, the covering of nerve fibers, according to the National Multiple Sclerosis Society (NMSS).

Fatigue is a very common symptom in people with MS—about 80 percent of patients struggle with it, according to the NMSS. The fatigue can significantly interfere with a person’s ability to function, even if they feel good otherwise. It can result from treatable medication side effects or other factors.

How to fix it: Specific treatments can help people with MS manage fatigue, including occupational therapy to simplify tasks at home, physical therapy to learn energy-saving ways to walk and do other day-to-day tasks, a regular exercise program, sleep regulation, and psychological interventions, per the NMSS.

This is another one to add to the category of “unlikely but worth knowing.” Cancer-related fatigue is incredibly common: At least 80 percent of people diagnosed with the condition experience it, according to the American Cancer Society (ACS). While cancer treatments could be to blame, people with cancer can also feel wiped out just from the disease itself. It can disrupt almost all metabolic pathways and sabotage the body's mitochondrial energy, Dr. Khan explains.

How to fix it: First, don't freak out that you have a serious health issue. But know that getting a proper diagnosis is important and, from there, proper treatment is needed, which usually involves chemotherapy, radiation therapy, surgery, or immunotherapy. The exact course depends on what type of cancer you have and how advanced it is. That's why seeing your doctor to rule it out is key if you haven't been able to shake your tiredness for a long time.

Here's the thing: Our body needs a range of nutrients and a certain number of calories per day to run at its normal capacity. Those numbers vary for everyone. "You need to not only make sure that you're providing yourself with a sufficient amount of calories per day to function, but also you need to be providing yourself with the appropriate nutrients," says Dr. Price.

If you don't, which can add to the fatigue, Dr. Price adds, even the smallest task feels monumental. You can also become constipated, says Dr. Price, which can contribute to the fatigue.

How to fix it: Eating a well-balanced diet and making sure that you’re eating enough fruits, vegetables, and proteins s essential to combatting fatigue, says Dr. Price. With the right nutrients, your body can create the energy you need to go about your day.

You’ve heard it before, but it’s true: Caffeine is a drug. Once your body gets accustomed to it, especially if you have coffee or soda every day, you become dependent on it. While it gives you a boost, caffeine also comes with highs and lows, and that can make you feel extremely tired, says Dr. Price. “Because you're burning calories when you have caffeine, you're moving at a faster pace, and you get to your peak of exhaustion much quicker,” she explains.

If you absolutely need coffee to get through your day, that should be a warning sign. "You should not have to have coffee in order to function, and something else is going on in your life that is causing you to be extremely fatigued," notes Dr. Price.

How to fix it: Switch up how you get your caffeine and start weaning yourself off of it. It'll take some adjustment, and you might get headaches at first, but once your body no longer depends on coffee, you'll feel a lot better.

While the medical world is still trying to figure out the long-term effects of COVID-19, we do know the toll the disease has on your body is that it makes you extremely exhausted, says Dr. Price. Your body tries to recuperate when you're infected—you lose your appetite, your muscles are extremely fatigued, and your body works in overdrive.

“Your body is doing the most to get you back to your normal self and to fight off COVID, so once you're done with it, you're not just going to bounce right back to yourself," says Dr. Price. “It's also been shown that the peak symptoms of COVID can sometimes be 14 days later. People must keep in mind this isn't a short illness. There are different and great severities of it."

Long COVID usually occurs two months after a person had COVID, says Dr. Price. If, two months after contracting the novel coronavirus, you’re still feeling worn out, it could be long COVID, she says.

How to fix it: The best way to protect yourself against COVID and the long-term complications is to get vaccinated and boosted and keep practicing good hygiene. If you suspect you have long COVID, visit your primary care provider and discuss your symptoms, Dr. Price advises. They can help you figure out your next steps.

Feeling tired once in a while is pretty normal—but experiencing constant fatigue isn’t. If you’re struggling, Dr. Khan recommends seeing your doctor. They’ll likely order several tests, including a physical exam and blood work, to figure out what, if anything, is happening.

"It's never too soon to go and talk to your doctor," says Dr. Price. What you thought might be minor, the doctor might look into more. "Or maybe you started googling things and, of course, Google will tell you you're dying, so talking to your doctor can give you relief and reassurance," Dr. Price adds.

The upside: the sooner you seek help, the sooner you can get answers—and get back to your rejuvenated and energetic self.

#5035. American Bitcoin’s $126M profit masks mining pressure as rivals turn to AI

The Eric Trump-backed miner reported $126 million in quarterly net income, largely driven by gains on its Bitcoin holdings, despite rising mining costs and shrinking margins.

American Bitcoin reported a quarterly net income of $126 million on Wednesday, but rising mining costs and falling profits from mining raise questions about its long-term strategy as major rivals pivot towards artificial intelligence.

The Eric Trump-backed company’s profit was driven by a $197.3 million noncash gain on its Bitcoin (BTC) holdings. Meanwhile, mining revenue fell 5.5% to $63.4 million from the previous quarter, while the cost to mine one BTC rose about 15% to $42,100.

Excluding gains from its Bitcoin holdings, American Bitcoin would have reported an operating loss of approximately $5.5 million, according to its financial statements. The company also recorded a separate $64.3 million loss on derivatives.

“Despite seasonal power cost pressures, we continued mining Bitcoin at a structural discount to spot,” American Bitcoin CEO Mike Ho said in Wednesday’s earnings announcement.

Earnings per share stood at $1.73, beating analysts’ consensus estimate of $0.26, according to Yahoo Finance data.

Bitcoin was trading at $76,119, down 5% over the past 24 hours as of 8:50 p.m. UTC, according to CoinGecko data. Shares of American Bitcoin (ABTC), meanwhile, closed Thursday down about 15% at $5.31.

The results highlight an interesting divide among Bitcoin miners: while American Bitcoin continues mining and holding BTC, competitors like Riot Platforms, MARA Holdings and Core Scientific are investing in AI to find new sources of revenue.

David Gamble, founder of asset management company Enigma Holdings, which focuses on Bitcoin mining and AI infrastructure, said American Bitcoin’s reported profit does not tell the full story of its mining business.

“Ultimately, mining Bitcoin below its market price doesn’t automatically mean you’re creating shareholder value,” Gamble told Cointelegraph.

American Bitcoin’s gross mining profit fell to $25.8 million from $33 million in the previous quarter. Its reported mining margin dropped to 41% from 49%, leaving the company with a smaller share of mining revenue after direct costs.

The company’s $63.4 million in quarterly revenue also fell short of analysts’ estimates of $74.35 million, according to Yahoo Finance data, a gap of nearly 15%.

The company mined 891 BTC during the quarter, down from 932 BTC in the previous three months. It also said higher electricity prices were the main reason mining costs increased.

“I don’t necessarily think their accumulation strategy is flawed, but I do think it raises questions about long-term sustainability,” Gamble said, pointing to increasing mining difficulty and the need to keep investing in newer and more efficient technology to compete.

Compared with the same period last year, revenue was nearly unchanged, falling from $64.2 million to $63.4 million. Reported profit, however, jumped from $3.5 million to $126 million, largely reflecting gains on the company’s Bitcoin holdings.

Related: Bitcoin miners face widening AI financing costs as lenders scrutinize deals

American Bitcoin’s strategy comes as competitors like Riot Platforms, MARA Holdings and Core Scientific rapidly expand into AI.

Riot is building large AI data centers, according to its latest earnings. Meanwhile MARA is expanding into AI while continuing to mine Bitcoin. Core Scientific, on the other hand, has already shifted much of its business toward AI data centers.

American Bitcoin, by contrast, continues building its BTC reserves, holding 8,427 BTC at the end of September, up from 3,418 BTC a year earlier.

“The real competitive advantage in this industry is shifting toward energy infrastructure,” Gamble said. “It’s no longer just about how much Bitcoin you can mine or how much hashrate you can deploy.”

Gamble also explained that Bitcoin miners and AI companies are competing for the same power supply, and AI data centers could make more money than Bitcoin mining in some areas.

“That doesn’t mean every mining facility can simply transition into AI,” he said. “You still have to account for depreciation, equipment replacement, financing costs, and the opportunity cost of deploying that capital elsewhere.”

#5033. 8 Best Hand Creams of 2026: 3-plus Years of Editor Testing and Reviews

From Joonbyrd to L’Occitane, here’s what formulas actually work.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

I’ll be the first to admit it: I spend a copious amount of money on spa treatments and refining my at-home skincare routine. From my $78 Isdin sunscreen and Paula’s Choice Exfoliant to biweekly facials, my focus has always been on achieving clear, wrinkle-free skin without injections. But after chatting with double board-certified dermatologist Serena Mraz, MD, I realized that I’ve been neglecting my hands for far too long.

As a New Yorker, I take the subway several times a day for work, events, and to meet up with friends. I’m also a germaphobe and rely on hand sanitizer or harsh soap in office and restaurant bathrooms after riding the train. As a result, my hands are inevitably dry, dull, and rough. That's not exactly the look I’m going for as our beauty editor. It also doesn’t help that Dr. Mraz says cumulative sun damage, including dark spots and wrinkles, is one of the biggest culprits in showing my age. Wait, what do you mean my age didn’t pause during the pandemic? “Your skin records sun exposure like your car tallies mileage,” dermatologist Hadley King, MD, previously told me about how skincare changes in your 40s. “You can't turn back the odometer on your car, and you can't turn back sun damage from your skin."

Hand creams provide instant hydration and support the skin barrier, helping me achieve the baby-soft skin of my dreams. I’ve noticed that investing in my favorites lets me reapply less frequently and use a smaller amount of product, ultimately trimming my cost per use. It’s beauty math, ladies!

Creams, such as L'Occitane and Beau Domaine, are the thickest and most nourishing type of hand care products. Dr. Mraz is a fan of creams because they’re packed with more lipids than water, which helps deliver the best moisture. The downside is that they take longer to rub into your hands and across larger areas like your arms.

Ointments, such as Aquaphor and Vaseline, are versatile, fragrance-free moisturizers that relieve dryness without causing irritation, says dermatologist and clinical professor of dermatology Gary Goldenberg, MD. They’re made with petrolatum to keep your skin moist, which helps prevent wounds from drying out and forming a scab. These are especially useful when you have super cracked skin.

Lotions are thinner and more fluid than creams. Some are even runny, but don’t worry: the right lotions still restore moisture for instant relief and prevent dryness. Notably, our best overall pick—Joonbyrd Wonder·Land Hand Lotion—has a luxurious consistency that feels and smells like it belongs in a spa. It’s so enjoyable, I use it all over my body and notice instant hydration without leaving residue on my clothes.

The dermatologists I consulted recommend moisturizing ingredients glycerin, shea butter, and cocoa butter—they can be found in many of our suggestions, like Aquaphor and Juara.

Hyaluronic acid is another standout that acts as a moisture magnet, holding up to 1,000 times its weight in water. It’s so epic that dermatologists recently told me you can even use hyaluronic acid on your hair and scalp.

As our fragrance-obsessed beauty editor, I also factored in the scent profiles. While all of these hand creams smell good, I also highlighted the key notes that make each one stand out. Homecourt's five impressive aromas—ranging from roses to citrus and aromatic—and Juara's Bali-inspired coconut blend are some of my favorites. If scents aren’t your thing, opt for Nécessaire. What it lacks in aroma, it makes up for in skin-nourishing and soothing ingredients, including ceramides and niacinamide.

To kick off this evaluation process, I went directly to medical experts about their no-fail hand creams.

Our team has been writing about hand creams since 2023. As our beauty editor, I refined our testing methodologies for 2026 to help you avoid buyer's remorse.

I opened the testing pool to 16 hand creams, but only eight were worth the hype. So how did I select them? In addition to ensuring these picks align with dermatological guidance, I compared 2026 launches (Joonbyrd) with reliable heavy-hitters, like L’Occitane and CeraVe. I also rummaged through the Women’s Health beauty closet and slathered on these hand creams. I started with my hands and explored how they work on different parts of my body, like my rough knees, elbows, and feet.

To narrow the list further, I used these hand creams twice daily for five months. I adored our best overall pick so much that I used it for an entire month straight before even writing this story because it’s genuinely that good.

I didn’t want the hand cream application to feel like a mundane process. To help you actually finish your next tube of hand cream, I evaluated each contender’s sensory experience. These factors included fragrance, texture, absorption time, finish, and if they left any residue on my gloves and tech.

Lastly, I considered each item’s cost relative to its product size and quality of ingredients and effectiveness. This final list includes a mix of affordable, mid-range, and splurge-worthy picks that provide varying levels of hydration and other cosmetic-enhancing benefits like lightening age spots.

Ready to make dry hands a thing of the past? Look no further than these eight expert-approved hand creams worthy of a spot in your routine.

As our beauty editor, I’m constantly searching for the next big thing. My new undisputed no. 1 hand cream hails from the U.K. and it quickly became my all-time favorite recommendation. Joonbyrd was developed by dermatologist Alexis Granite, MD, who is certified in America and the U.K. Given her elite medical background—Dr. Granite was a clinical instructor at New York-Presbyterian/Weill-Cornell—my expectations for her line were high.

Joonbyrd offers two hand care products: the featured Wonder·Land Hand Lotion and the Confetti Sky Hand Treatment. TBH, the latter is compact and more travel-friendly, but I genuinely love the Hand Lotion so much that I apply it to my body. My picky, scent-averse mom also found the sophisticated warm and sweet gourmand notes appealing.

Aroma aside, the formulation and ingredient list are on point. The fast-absorbing, nourishing lotion is made with all my favorite heavy hitters: ceramides, amino acids, and niacinamide. They’re basically a trifecta of skincare goodness that repair my skin barrier and hydrate.

Joonbyrd says this hand lotion moisturizes for up to 48 hours. However, I’m a frequent handwasher, so I can’t speak to going two full days without soap and water on my skin. Personally, I noticed my hands felt softer after a week of consistent reapplication, and I can use this lotion twice daily afterward.

I initially tested it for one month straight before comparing it to other picks. (More on that shortly!) Since then, I’ve been reaching for it almost daily for a year. It offers long-lasting hydration and skin barrier support. Joonbyrd is so effective at leaving my skin softer and more supple that even acquaintances have noticed how soft my hands feel.

To ensure others received a fair assessment during the second month of my testing process, I evaluated how Joonbyrd compared with other options.

Joonbyrd wins over the French staple, as it’s lighter and features a modern formula that strengthens the skin barrier. L’Occitane remains a beauty classic, but if you want something new that you can apply all over your body, opt for Joonbyrd.

Go with the latter if you’re a price-conscious shopper or just super sensitive to scents. Hey, it happens to me during the spring and summer, so I totally get it! Joonbyrd is luxurious—the pretty packaging elevates my vanity setup, and the green, textured exterior helps prevent it from rolling off my table.

Joonbyrd is a non-sticky lotion that absorbs quickly, leaving no pesky residue on my belongings. It’s your best bet when your hands require barrier support and nourishment. On the flip side, Aquaphor feels heavy compared to Joonbyrd’s formulation, but not as greasy as Vaseline. (More on that soon—promise!)

Here’s where things get tougher. HomeCourt offers an aspirational, Insta-famous brand vibe, but with hydrating ingredients that enhance my skin. I also like HomeCourt partially because it offers five fragrances ranging from floral to aromatic and citrus. It provides just enough hydration after washing my dishes or hands. Woo! But if I could only own one hand cream, I’d opt for Joonbyrd since it’s cosmetically elegant and effective. FWIW, HomeCourt makes a body care collection if you prefer. I’m also so obsessed with the 2026 Skincare Award-winning Exfoliating Hand Soap.

Both hand creams provide ample skin-replenishing goodness. While the fragrance-free Nécessaire Hand Cream is helpful for anyone with a sensitive nose and skin, I do miss having access to the line’s signature scents at my disposal.

Overall, Joonbyrd is the best hand cream to beat in 2026. I’ll continue using this nourishing lotion for its standout ingredients, luxurious feeling, and impressive short- and long-term performance. Plus, the packaging is pretty and adds a fun, feminine touch to my vanity. The container has a nice weight and features a lightly cushioned textured green exterior to help prevent accidental slippage.

CeraVe makes a strong case that you don’t have to splurge to enjoy a solid hand cream. To start, it contains derm-approved and barrier-supporting ingredients, including niacinamide, hyaluronic acid, ceramides, and dimethicone. It also boasts a fragrance-free formula that doesn’t irritate my sensitive skin.

I genuinely enjoy this hand cream and plan to keep it in my rotation. But here’s the caveat: It requires more frequent reapplication than thicker creams. But at just $10 a tube, it’s by far the most affordable recommendation, making it a major win in my book. Pick this hand cream if you’re shopping on a budget and don’t want to skimp on moisture and barrier repair.

Aquaphor and Vaseline are similar drugstore staples—they both use petroleum to lock in moisture and repair the skin barrier. #5031. OpenAI Promises to Foot AI’s Power Bill After Voter Revolt. Who Enforces It?

OpenAI Promises to Foot AI’s Power Bill After Voter Revolt. Who Enforces It?

OpenAI promised Thursday that neighbors of its future AI campuses won't foot the bill for their electricity needs, two days after voters dealt data center expansion a blow at the polls. It also pledged public energy and water figures and limits on secrecy agreements with officials.

Its National Community Compact covers future campuses (a fancy rebranding for the term AI data centers) OpenAI both owns and operates, according to the company’s announcement. A separate policy blueprint asks legislators and regulators to apply similar requirements across the industry.

“We will pay the full cost of powering our compute campuses and ensure local residents do not foot the bill for our energy needs,” OpenAI said in its “national community compact.”

It also promises financial assurances before costly grid upgrades, so other customers aren't left paying if projects shrink or fail. Each AI data center would get a water plan, annual progress reports and independent noise studies.

The national compact does not describe penalties for noncompliance, a dispute process or independent verification of electricity and water figures. Residents could read OpenAI’s disclosures, but the document does not explain how they could compel corrections or recover losses.

The ownership restriction matters, too. Partners including Oracle build and own much of the infrastructure reserved for OpenAI, Reuters reported. The company has not said its national compact automatically covers those partner-owned facilities.

The proposed blueprint goes further on electricity costs. “Policymakers should direct state public utility commissions to create large-load tariffs that require large electricity consumers to pay the incremental costs they create,” it says.

A large-load tariff is a special electricity rate for major users such as data centers. The blueprint also urges mandatory reporting, narrow limits on officials’ NDAs and provisions letting governments reclaim tax breaks when developers miss agreed commitments.

The announcement follows a bruising election for the industry. Voters backed restrictions on data centers in multiple states, while candidates pushing for tighter oversight won key races. The results basically send a message to OpenAI and the broader AI industry that communities want a bigger say in how these facilities are built and how their operations affect the people living nearby.

OpenAI says every future campus it owns and operates will receive its own local compact and an annual public progress report.

#5029. I Tried Two New Therabody Devices. Now I Have a Tension-Melting Routine

Combining heat, massage, and targeted percussive therapy helps me decompress from the day.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

I carry my stress in my shoulders and my neck. I always have. It’s the last quarter of the year, when my job responsibilities tend to ramp up, and my family responsibilities have increased this year, too. Recently, I started running regularly again to help find some me-time, but instead of releasing tension, the activity has left my traps and neck stiff. No amount of meditation or yoga is releasing these burdens that have nested into my shoulders, upper back, and neck.

Luckily, Therabody needed someone to try its newest massagers: ThermNeck & Upper Back and ThermFace Massage. I was their girl. Full disclosure: I’m a Theragun fan. It’s been helping me recover from workouts and race training for years. Products that target my stress zone sounded promising—and much-needed. I love my Theragun, but it can be unwieldy when I try to reach those areas myself. So I began working the two Therabody massagers into my after-work wellness routine. Here’s how it went.

First, I started with ThermFace Massage. When I initially heard about it, I wasn’t sure what to expect—or whether I’d like it. I live with migraine, so I was cautious about the prospect of vibration around my head.

ThermFace Massage may look just like a smaller Theragun, but it isn’t. It’s Therabody’s first heated percussive massage device specifically engineered to handle the delicate muscles of the face, jaw, and neck. It has 5-millimeter amplitude, three speeds, three heat levels, and four specialized attachments—Heat, Cone, Dampener, and Wedge—that allows you to customize the experience. And unlike the Theragun, ThermFace Massage has a 45-degree ergonomic shape that makes it easier to navigate all contours of the jaw, face, and neck.

The first time I used it, I chose the softest attachment and started on the right side of my neck. I turned it on at the lowest speed and moved slowly, paying close attention to how my body responded. The sensation was targeted but not aggressive. Once I felt comfortable, I guided it along the muscles of my jaw, where I hadn’t fully realized I was holding tension.

That became my first lesson: Stress doesn’t always announce itself. Sometimes it shows up as a clenched jaw during a difficult email, a tight face at the end of a long day, or stiffness that seems to travel from the jaw into the neck.

Next, I tested the heat attachment. ThermFace Massage offers three temperature settings ranging from 40°C to 55°C. This allowed me start conservatively rather than jump straight to the warmest option, which has since become my favorite setting. The combination of adjustable heat and focused percussion made the routine feel purposeful: I’m taking care of my overlooked, overworked muscles.

Because I live with migraine, I remain deliberate about where and how I use the device, but I haven’t experienced an increase in episodes while using ThermFace Massage. Anyone managing a health condition should check with a medical professional before introducing a new device into their routine, as I did before I started this trial.

Next came the ThermNeck & Upper Back, a cordless wearable designed to target the neck, trapezius muscles, and upper back. If ThermFace Massage helped me notice how much tension I carried around my jaw, ThermNeck & Upper Back went straight to the place where I already knew my stress lived.

It combines five complementary therapies in one device: kneading massage, heat, far-infrared, near-infrared LED light, and Theragun percussive therapy. Two built-in Theraguns are positioned over the trapezius muscles (located in the upper back), and the device includes three percussive speeds, three kneading patterns, and three heat levels.

I slipped it over my shoulders while seated on my sofa and used the adjustable strap to fine-tune the placement and pressure. It’s wearable, but not completely hands-free; resting my hands in the straps allowed me to control how firmly the device sat against my muscles.

That adjustability mattered. After a run, I may want a different intensity than I do after eight hours at my computer. Some evenings, I’m looking for targeted percussion. On others, soothing heat and kneading massage are more appealing. Depending on what my body needs on a given day, I can tailor my time with the device.

ThermNeck & Upper Back should be used while seated or standing, not while lying down. I found sitting on the sofa to be the ideal setup—comfortable enough to decompress, but upright enough to keep the device properly positioned.

Now when I get home from work, I change into comfy clothes, make dinner for my family, then slip on ThermNeck & Upper Body and watch television while the device kneads away the stress of the day that has built up in my neck and upper back. Five minutes is all it takes to transition from responsibility mode to evening mode, making me more relaxed and calm for the time that means the most to me: family time.

The hour before bed, I brush my teeth, wash my face, use my TheraFace Mask Glo (told you I was a Therabody fan), then retire to my bed. There, I use the ThermFace Massage (usually the Heat attachment) to massage my jaw and neck for a couple of minutes on each side, followed by reading and then sleep.

While none of the devices will erase my deadlines or family obligations, they do help with my running-related stiffness and ease the tension in the places where those pressures land. This routine has also created a better awareness around my head, neck, and shoulders: Am I clenching my jaw? Are my shoulders creeping toward my ears? Have I moved away from my screen recently?

Meditation and yoga still have a place in my life. But sometimes my stress feels less like a thought I can breathe through and more like a knot sitting directly on top of my shoulders. This new routine allows me to use targeted heat and massage to help loosen that knot with a few intentional minutes at the end of the day.

Carey Rossi is the Senior News Editor for Prevention and Prevention.com. You can follow her @CareyRossi.

#5028. Here’s what happened in crypto today

Need to know what happened in crypto today? Here is the latest news on daily trends and events impacting Bitcoin price, blockchain, DeFi, Web3 and crypto regulation.

Today in crypto, Bitcoin fell below $76,000 as US stocks reversed their post-election gains, France’s National Assembly rejected a budget revenue bill containing proposed crypto tax measures and Democrats threatened investigations into President Donald Trump’s crypto ventures.

Bitcoin (BTC) fell below $76,000 on Thursday as risk assets abruptly reversed their US midterm elections rally. After making swift gains on the back of a Democrat sweep of the US House of Representatives and Senate, equities saw sharp selling on Thursday as traders rotated out of tech.

Crypto markets liquidated nearly $500 million in positioning in the first hour of the US trading session, per data from CoinGlass, with 24-hour total liquidations nearing $850 million.

Now targeting $76,000, BTC/USD approached the bottom of its one-month trading range. The pair risked losing support at the 50-week simple moving average (SMA), currently at $75,733.

In his latest analysis, crypto trader Rekt Capital noted that Bitcoin was repeating behavior from May, when a period of rangebound moves around $80,000 subsequently gave way to a breakdown and new macro lows of $57,000 in late June.

The French National Assembly rejected the 2027 state budget’s revenue section late Wednesday, removing crypto-related amendments from the version of the bill sent to the Senate.

The Assembly rejected the revenue section by a vote of 458–0 at first reading, with two abstentions, according to the official voting record. The revenue section covers government income, such as taxes.

After the rejection, the government will send the Senate the original budget text, without the crypto-related amendments adopted by lawmakers.

The proposals could still be reintroduced in the Senate and would need to survive the remaining budget process to become law.

Faustine Fleuret, global head of public affairs at lending protocol Morpho, told Cointelegraph before the vote that rejection of the revenue section could “result in the rejection of the entire bill in the National Assembly, including provisions adopted by way of amendments.”

Democratic lawmakers said Big Tech executives should expect a wave of subpoenas, along with investigations into US President Donald Trump’s business circle when the new Congress takes office in January.

As Democrats secured control of Congress in a sweeping victory on Tuesday, Democratic lawmakers were already firing warning shots at the Trump family and tech executives close to the US president.

“They are going to need to plant new forests for all of the subpoenas we’ll be printing,” said US Senator Ed Markey, who sits on the Senate Commerce Committee, referring to Big Tech executives.

US Representative Robert Garcia, the expected incoming House Oversight Committee chairman, said he plans to take on “the Trump Crime Family, corporate corruption, DHS and ICE abuses, and the cover-up of the Epstein files,” adding a two-word warning: “Lawyer up.”

The warnings follow a mid-term election campaign in which Democrats challenged Big Tech’s growing political influence and the rapid expansion of AI, raising concerns about data centers’ demands on electricity, safeguards for AI products and tech executives’ ties to Trump.

#5027. Bitcoin Fixes This? Paraguay President Resigns After Bank Lockout Hits 60% of Country

Bitcoin Fixes This? Paraguay President Resigns After Bank Lockout Hits 60% of Country

Paraguayan President Santiago Peña announced his resignation Thursday after the collapse of digital lender Ueno Bank left roughly three million customers unable to access their accounts, turning a banking failure into a political emergency.

The announcement did not end the confrontation. Paraguay’s lower house voted 78-0 to impeach Peña minutes later, sending the charges to the Senate as Vice President Pedro Alliana prepared to take over, per AP.

The bank’s shutdown has also revived an argument familiar to Bitcoin holders: Money held by a financial intermediary is only as accessible as that institution allows. Self-custodied Bitcoin works differently, although it comes with risks of its own.

As the Bitcoin OGs would say, “Bitcoin fixes this.”

Paraguay’s central bank placed Ueno into resolution after regulators found its solvency ratio had fallen below half the legal minimum. The process, announced on November 3, followed accounting adjustments to the bank’s financial statements and left its app, cards, and transfers out of service.

Ueno claimed around 2.9 million customers, more than 60% of Paraguay’s adults. It also held roughly $800 million in public-sector deposits, including pension and health funds, according to AP, complicating an already painful freeze on ordinary savings and wages.

Peña previously held a stake in a company linked to Ueno’s owners, a relationship that fueled allegations of conflicts of interest. He has denied wrongdoing and said he sold his interest in March 2025, according to Reuters.

“My greatest wish is that the return of those funds be guaranteed, that the Deposit Guarantee Fund fulfill its purpose,” Peña said in his resignation address. The Deposit Guarantee Fund would be the equivalent of the FDIC in the United States.

Mensaje del Presidente de la República, Santiago Peña, al pueblo paraguayo. pic.twitter.com/RBz6IfFV9L

— Santiago Peña (@SantiPenap) November 5, 2026

Outside Congress, the anger was less diplomatic. “Santiago Peña and the Colorado Party have stolen all our savings,” protester Luis Pineda told AP. “We are fed up with 70 years of plunder.”

The central bank says Ueno’s failure is an isolated case, not evidence of a broader banking collapse. It has announced a deposit guarantee of up to 228.3 million guaraníes per depositor, roughly $36,000, with initial payments targeting eligible accounts holding no more than 10 million guaraníes.

Bitcoin was born during another banking crisis. Its first block, mined in January 2009, included a newspaper headline about a second bailout for British banks, a historical detail long cited by supporters who distrust reliance on financial intermediaries.

A Bitcoin holder who controls a wallet’s private keys can authorize transfers from the wallet without asking a third party, like a bank, to unlock an account. That is the principle behind the crypto saying “not your keys, not your coins,” and the distinction from leaving Bitcoin on an exchange that can itself freeze withdrawals.

But Bitcoin is not perfect. Self-custody has its own risks: Forget your keys or lose them and there is no way you can recover your funds, there is nobody who can assist you, and no law enforcement agency who can do anything for you.

Paraguay’s central bank said Thursday its first guarantee payments would go to depositors with up to 10 million guaraníes, no Ueno debts and an account at another financial institution.

The Pacer Fleece features a smooth exterior and a warm brushed interior for movement in the cold. Another bonus? The center-seam-free design eliminates discomfort and prevents any unwanted lines, like camel toes.

Fleece is a soft, synthetic fabric usually made from a polyester blend, often mixed with spandex, nylon, or cotton. Designed to mimic wool's insulating properties, fleece is lighter, more breathable, and dries faster, making it ideal for staying warm. Plus, fleece wicks away moisture and dries quickly, keeping you cozy even if you’re caught in rain or snow. Think of it like wearing a warm hug on a cold day.

When it comes to fleece leggings, the fabric blend is key. Most leggings are made from a mix of polyester and spandex, which delivers the perfect blend of warmth, stretch, and comfort. We’ll give you a quick breakdown of why these materials are key here:

Polyester traps heat, making it an ideal blend for staying warm without bulk. Compared to natural fibers like cotton, polyester dries quickly. WH editors love wearing polyester-fleece leggings on long walks since they stay warm without making you feel sweaty. It’s super lightweight yet strong, meaning you won’t feel like the material is clinging to your body. Polyester also helps leggings retain their shape over time, so stretching, sagging, and pilling are likely a thing of the past.

Nylon is a popular material in fleece leggings because of its strength, flexibility, and smooth texture. It resists wear and tear, which helps keep leggings looking brand new after every wash. Nylon also offers water resistance, which is key for outdoor activities in light rain or snow.

Spandex (also known as elastane or Lycra) is the go-to fabric for stretch and flexibility. It fits snugly and moves with you, giving you maximum comfort during high-impact activities like yoga, running, and dance. While it’s not as durable on its own, spandex is often blended with other fabrics (like nylon or polyester) to create leggings that are both stretchy and long-lasting.

Here’s the thing: Fleece leggings with thicker fabric may feel more restrictive than traditional leggings, so if you plan to wear them for high-impact activities, opting for a bigger size might be best. Look for four-way stretch fabrics like spandex for flexibility and comfort. If you're between sizes, choose based on your desired fit—size down for a sculpted look (read: tighter) or up for a relaxed, lounge-friendly feel.

Fleece leggings are perfect for workouts, offering warmth and insulation while keeping the stretch and comfort you need for full range of motion. “The leggings should have a good amount of stretch while fitting snugly, and maintaining their shape throughout the day,” says fashion designer Kadeem Alphanso Fyffe. Look for features like a wide, high-rise waistband for a secure fit.

As writers who've tried more than our fair share of leggings, we know what it takes to make a great pair. To earn a place on this list, these fleece leggings had to meet strict criteria. We analyzed everything from fabric quality and warmth to stretch, fit, and breathability. Were they soft and supportive? Did they hold their shape after multiple washes? Could they withstand the elements without pilling or wearing thin? We examined every seam, ensuring each recommendation is a worthy investment for your winter wardrobe and beyond.

We also consulted two style experts, who shared their favorite pairs and provided insight on what to consider when shopping for fleece leggings. They emphasized that materials, stretch, and overall style matter, since they can make or break a pair of leggings (and pants, for that matter).

#5023. Claude Gets Personal Finance—Anthony Pompliano Accuses Anthropic of Copying His Startup

Claude Gets Personal Finance—Anthony Pompliano Accuses Anthropic of Copying His Startup

Anthropic launched a personal finance feature for Claude on Thursday, letting users connect bank accounts and ask the chatbot what they have spent, what bills are due, and how much money they have left.

The announcement came with an accusation from crypto investor Anthony Pompliano, who says Anthropic tried to get inside information from his competing startup, Silvia, months before the rollout.

Claude can then summarize spending, spot duplicate charges or unexpected fees, explain interest payments, and build charts or bill calendars. Anthropic's example shows a user asking where their money went this month, then following up to find out what remains before payday.

"Access is read-only, and Claude can't move, manage, or invest your money," Anthropic wrote on X. "Like the rest of Claude, Personal Finance has no ads or sponsored recommendations," the company added.

The assistant can see balances, credit limits, account names and types, the last four digits of account numbers, and up to 24 months of transactions from accounts users approve. According to Anthropic's documentation, conversations inside the Personal Finance hub are not used to train its models, but account balances may lag and AI-generated figures should be checked against bank records.

Also, how Anthropic tracks its users’ financial data and uses it beyond model training is not completely clear—and this is extremely valuable information for big data companies.

Pompliano, whose Silvia product bills itself as a personal AI chief financial officer, posted a lengthy complaint on X shortly after Claude's announcement. He alleged that Anthropic personnel began approaching Silvia engineers in April, supposedly because they were interested in the startup's growth and computing needs.

"Anthropic was fishing for details on how we built our product and why it was so popular," he wrote. Pompliano also described an alleged steak dinner involving Anthropic representatives, claiming someone told the Silvia team that the meal was on CEO Dario Amodei's tab.

His engineers declined to divulge sensitive information, he claimed, adding that someone at Anthropic warned his team about the upcoming launch days earlier.

"Today, Anthropic launched a Claude wrapper that is masquerading as a personal finance product," Pompliano wrote. He also claimed Silvia's computing costs are roughly 97% lower when using its own models and hardware instead of frontier AI models, which most likely mean they are using a local model, likely finetuned for the startup’s specific purpose.

That said, Anthropic was not the first general-purpose chatbot maker to move into personal finance, either. OpenAI launched similar bank connections for ChatGPT in May, also using Plaid for read-only access to financial information, and Agents like Hermes, Open Claw, or Grok Bot can also do pretty similar tasks given the proper skills, permissions, and instructions.

Anthropic says Personal Finance is a first step and is exploring partnerships with licensed professionals and regulated financial firms. For now, the beta is restricted to U.S. customers on three paid Claude plans.

#5020. Jaylen Brown Is Keeping His Boston Skyrise Apartment—and Its 18th-Century Harp Too

Jaylen Brown has an 18th-century harp sitting in his living room, and that harp rules. Having a harp in your home as a newly 30-year-old man, with a professional harpist who appears when the mood strikes said 30-year-old man, is an outstanding use of free will.

“Most of the time I use my harp, it’s just me and my chef,” Brown says. A memory raced through my head: me at the end of my twenties, with a harp (broken) in my unit. And how central it would be to the social hours of 10 p.m. to 4 a.m.

Brown’s harp is not that. “It’s almost like therapy sessions,” he says.

Jaylen Brown with his cat Forky in the speakeasy of his Boston apartment design by Linda Zarifi of Zarifi Design. The project construction is by Core Group Boston, and the art advisory is by Miranda Girard. The sofa is by Charles Zana from The Invisible Collection and the ottoman is also from The Invisible Collection.

The artwork in the speakeasy is by Francisco Valverde and flanked with sconces by Apparatus. The curtain fabric is by Dedar, and the star ceiling lights are by UFO Lighting.

The wallcovering in the speakeasy is by Gregorius Pineo.

He’s a different dude in a way that makes him singular, both as a member of the National Basketball Association and as a young and famous Black man in the United States of America.

On July 1, 2026, hundreds of television talkers said “the basketball world has been turned upside down,” because Brown’s years as a Celtic were over. After nine years as half of Boston’s dynamic duo with Jayson Tatum, he was traded to Philadelphia. “It’s no beef,” Jaylen politely told reporters after a victory, beard absolutely glistening. “Might be a little lamb chops and gravy. He should stay ready at all times.” 23 days later, Brown’s new teammate became LeBron James, creating one of the strongest starting fives in NBA history. You love to see someone find a nice new gig. But as for where he rests his head, Brown held on to his place in Boston.

In the foyer, the walls are painted in Ashland Slate by Benjamin Moore. The custom table holds Brown’s championship trophy.

In the living area, the Hamilton sofa by Minotti surrounds coffee tables by Carlyle Collective. The floor lamp is by Tom Dixon and the harp is antique.

“I grew up here, age 19 to 29,” he says. “I developed here in Boston as a young man.” The feeling is mutual; October 24, his birthday, was named Jaylen Brown Day by the city. But the crib itself is doing a lot of the pulling. “There’s been other places, but this one–I created it, it was crafted by my vision,” he says. “This one felt the most like home.”

He hired a team to bring his dreams to life. Linda Zarifi, of Zarifi Design, was his interior designer and collaborator. “The first thing he said was ‘I don’t want a dining table,’” Zarifi says. “And I’m like, ‘Where are you going to…eat?’”

The answer was a 22-foot banquette, built for most home activities. “I wanted to open the space up. I wanted gold. I wanted a see-through wine cellar. She went and found it all,” Brown says.

The custom dining banquette is upholstered in Crescendo Suede by Kravet and the glass and metal tables are also custom by Jamie Cumming. The TG-10 Sling dining chairs are by Gratz Industries and the wall sconces are by Gubi. Inside the frame on the wall is a proclamation from the city of Boston for Jaylen Brown Day.

Brown in the dining 22-foot banquette playing chess.

The result is a 4,000 square-foot apartment overlooking Boston’s Financial District. Brown purchased the downtown space at the end of 2023 and reworked it over the last two years. Outside, the apartment is a brief 15 minute walk from the TD Garden, home to Brown’s former team, the Boston Celtics. Inside, Italian marble bathroom floors, 24-karat fixtures that Brown calls “jewelry for the bathroom,” a custom suede pool table, a party-ready speakeasy, and that see-through wine cellar.

There’s also the medical hyperbaric chamber one step from his bed, one of the truest signs that he is a single man.

Jaylen is figuring it out in real time, and he’s letting us watch and listen.

The trophy case in the speakeasy is a custom design by Zarifi and fabricated by Jamie Cumming of Loki Custom Furniture.

A shelf in the speakeasy holds a collection of Brown’s memorabilia.

A chandelier by John Pomp hangs over the bar area with stools by Blackman Cruz upholstered in a leather by Holly Hunt.

Kashmir barstools from Resident tuck under the kitchen counter. The flushmounts are from RH and the kitchen cabinets are painted in Cheating Heart by Benjamin Moore.

They say he’s not new to this. In 2020, five days after George Floyd was murdered, he drove 15 hours from Boston to our hometown of Atlanta to lead a march. The same guy who took the swooshes off his Nikes and now has his own shoe company (with custom closets by Molteni&C to house his own collection). And the same guy who uses this home to stream with Lil Baby and to display art and artifacts dedicated to his family.

“The things that he would splurge on are the things that mattered most to him,” Zarifi says. There’s the commissioned portrait in cracked glass by Simon Berger of his grandmother in the foyer. “For him, his flex was his harp.”

Brown seated on a bench in the primary bedroom. The custom bed is upholstered in a fabric by Mokum Textiles. The pendants by Apparatus hang over custom nightstands flanking the bed.

“I had them recreate the bedding and the comforter of the Four Seasons,” Brown says. The bedding is custom from Crem & Co. Brown’s hyperbaric chamber is beside the bed.

“I love the tempered glass,” Brown says. The closet design is the Glis Closet System by Molteni&C. The pendant light is by Henge.

The custom vanity in the primary bathroom is flanked with sconces by Articolo. The fittings are by Dornbracht.

The tub and counters are topped in a Pietra Grey stone and the ceiling is painted Ashland Slate by Benjamin Moore.

The Sally armchair in the primary bedroom is by Minotti and covered in Holly Hunt fabric.

He hasn’t taken lessons yet, good for no more than an impassioned glissando. What he does do, however, is soak in the sounds from Li Shan Tan, the principal harp for the New Haven Symphony Orchestra, who plucks from his palace. “From time to time, when she is not around, I go and I try to recreate some sounds.”

#5017. Core Scientific to quit Bitcoin mining by year-end as AI business surges

The former Bitcoin mining heavyweight plans to become a pure-play data center provider in 2027, with mining now accounting for just 7.5% of quarterly revenue.

Core Scientific said it expects to exit Bitcoin mining by the end of 2026 as it shifts its business toward artificial intelligence and high-performance computing infrastructure.

“We expect to complete the wind-down during the fourth quarter and enter 2027 as a pure-play, high-density colocation provider,” said Core Scientific chief financial officer Jim Nygaard during a Q3 earnings call on Thursday.

Core Scientific had previously disclosed in a July quarterly filing that hosted mining would end by Dec. 31 as it continued to wind down its self-mining operations. The comment establishes a targeted completion date for its complete Bitcoin mining exit.

Its latest quarterly results show how far the business has shifted. Third-quarter self-mining revenue fell to $12.3 million from $57.4 million a year earlier, while revenue from hosting other miners dropped to $5.2 million from $8.7 million.

Bitcoin mining generated just $17.5 million, or 7.5%, of Core Scientific’s $232.9 million in third-quarter revenue, compared with $215.4 million from colocation services.

The company had nearly 50% fewer miners online at the end of September than at the end of 2025, according to Nygaard.

Core Scientific’s move into AI hosting took shape in 2024 after signing an initial hosting agreement with CoreWeave in March that year, followed by a much larger deal announced in June.

At the time, Core Scientific presented AI hosting as an expansion alongside its mining business. Its June 2024 announcement emphasized preserving Bitcoin mining capacity.

By December 2025, The Wall Street Journal reported that Core Scientific was already targeting to wind down its Bitcoin mining operations by 2028.

Related: American Bitcoin’s $126M profit masks mining pressure as rivals turn to AI

Mining economics added pressure in the months following.

Core Scientific recorded $266.5 million in mining-related asset impairments in the first quarter of 2026, citing weaker Bitcoin prices, historically low hashprice and falling digital asset mining equipment values in the secondary market.

During the second quarter, it terminated its mining equipment agreement with Block and Proto, recording a $41.9 million loss as part of the transition.

The planned exit comes as the US election results introduce fresh uncertainty for companies expanding AI infrastructure.

Opponents of data-center development were among the winners of the Democrats’ sweeping midterm gains, Reuters reported Wednesday.

The backlash centers partly on electricity bills and the demands large facilities place on local resources.

Cointelegraph reached out to Core Scientific for comment.

#5016. This Picture-Perfect Suburban Kitchen Is an Argument for Maxed-Out Storage, Smart Appliances, and Custom Furniture

The house had everything a young family could want: classic Georgian architecture, a picturesque setting in East Glenview, Illinois, and a neighborhood where kids ride bikes and golden retrievers lounge in the sunshine. For a Chicago couple with three young sons, it checked nearly every box. The kitchen, however, was another story. Its awkward layout, dated appliances, and surprisingly scant storage made it feel ill-equipped for the demands of a family of five.

Fortunately, the problem was one that could be solved. Elizabeth Smolcich, principal interior designer of Glenview-based firm Mason & Brass, saw its potential. “We knew we’d have to do a heavy lift to make the kitchen feel like ours,” says homeowner Marie Gould. “Elizabeth had a vision for it that made it much more functional and kid-friendly for our family.”

Kitchen layout, before and after. A wider doorway improves the flow between kitchen and living room, while removal of a corner booth created space for a larger island and the potential for more seating.

Homeowner Marie Gould with her sons in the renovated kitchen

Smolcich started by widening the opening between the kitchen and living room just enough to let Gould easily eyeball what her boys, ages seven, six, and three, are up to while she’s cooking. And removing an awkwardly positioned corner booth, one that was too small to seat four comfortably but obtrusive enough to impede the 450-square-foot room’s flow, gave the designer enough space to expand and center a new kitchen island.

This island, topped with smoky quartzite, provides plenty of counter space for prepping meals or rolling out dough, and even has enough room for the boys to perch on when they want a close-up view of the action. Above the island, Smolcich installed schoolhouse-style fluted glass pendants, which the couple loved for their timeless appeal. The most significant addition came in the form of a custom banquette that looks built into the island but is actually a movable bench. The family slides this upholstered piece to one side of their dining table, which can expand from 66 inches to 96 inches, when they’re hosting a crowd. (They’re expecting more than 40 friends and family for Thanksgiving this year.)

This is a family that loves to entertain, says the designer. A double oven, then, was a necessity, as was a serious range that allows Gould to sear a steak with restaurant-quality char. Gould discovered the ultimate time-saving mom hack with her smart SKS range, which features a rapid preheat function and can boil a pot of water for pasta in just 60 seconds. It has both an induction cooktop to prevent tiny hands from burns as well as four gas burners that satisfy even the pickiest of eaters.

Two ovens were a necessity for the homeowners’ busy household. Brizo Rook faucets and Visual Comfort Parkington sconces, both in brass, warm up the space.

The designer flanked the stove with open shelving to keep oils and seasonings within easy reach. The dual gas and induction SKS range features Smart Knob technology.

“They’re big on texture,” says Gould of her family. “They love a sear and a proper crust, which is no problem with this.” (That said, she says she personally appreciates the range’s sous vide capability best, although the SKS Smart Knobs, with LED screens for each knob to monitor heat levels and time remaining, are a close second.)

Over the range Smolcich installed hidden, integrated built-in storage to hold Gould’s go-to oils and seasonings. “We took advantage of any and all real estate,” explains the designer. “We can make anything beautiful. That’s not the hard part of what we do. The hard part is making it wildly functional. If you feel like you have to go out of your way to retrieve something, you’re never going to love it.”

Designer Elizabeth Smolcich’s mood board of furniture, lighting, and surfaces

Hence the dual SKS dishwashers staggered from the Kohler prep sink in the island to the main Kohler sink (with a custom cutting board) on the perimeter; the pantry wall (painted Benjamin Moore Black Iron); an appliance garage (housing Gould’s toaster, blenders, mixer, and coffee maker); and a beverage bar with a 24-inch second fridge stocked with her kids’ juice boxes.

Prominently displayed is a treasured side-by-side picture frame with a photo of Gould’s parents baking with her oldest son next to a handwritten copy of their family’s banana bread recipe.

“That’s what this kitchen is to them,” says Smolcich. “In a few years, those boys will be sitting at that banquette doing their homework. We took this big house with a kitchen built for just a few people and made it one for a family.”

The beverage bar includes an under-the-counter refrigerator where the kids can grab their juice boxes.

#5010. 65 Chicago Interior Designers to Know From the AD PRO Directory

Based in America’s third-largest city, these Chicago interior designers and architects have widely varied styles: from bold and bursting with color to chic, minimal, and restrained. These talents from the AD PRO Directory are at the top of their game, attracting the Windy City’s most discerning clients and tackling everything from glamorous Gold Coast penthouses to modern farmhouse-style family retreats in Hinsdale. Whether you’re contemplating a new build, a full renovation, or just a refresh, scroll on to discover 65 studios to hire for your next project in Chicago and beyond, and visit their Directory profiles for more images, information, and contact details to get in touch.

In a Lincoln Park home, Abrams Valenti Interiors set the tone in a bedroom by placing a screen from the client’s previous home behind a Dmitriy & Co. Brampton bed.

Precision and artistry go hand in hand in the designs of Abrams Valenti Interiors. Founded by principal Michael Abrams nearly 25 years ago, the firm offers residential design and interior architecture services for clients across the United States, from a contemporary Miami home to a mountain retreat in Utah. Interiors are imbued with comfort and sophistication and are intentionally designed to enhance the lives of those who occupy them. Abrams and his team pride themselves on personalized client service, and each project has a dedicated creative team that works with Abrams to bring the home to life.

Modern elegance meets traditional charm in this downtown Chicago primary bedroom, featuring bold patterns and lush green accents. Designed by Aishah Nisar Interior Design, the space is beautifully elevated with an ornate mother-of-pearl and green crystal chandelier that serves as an opulent centerpiece to the urban retreat.

Aishah Nisar designs homes that are meant to be lived in and enjoyed. The founder and creative director of Aishah Nisar Interior Design focuses on creating a balance of practicality and style in her spaces, adding unique details to showcase the clients’ personal style. Nisar and her team work hard to understand their clients’ unique needs and to guide them through the whole process. The firm works on projects throughout the Chicago area, including renovations and new builds, and Nisar brings a wealth of knowledge about design and construction thanks to her experience working as a consultant for builders.

For a Chicago gut renovation, Aleksandra Nova Design created a home thats feels both seductive and sophisticated—and is perfect for entertaining.

Aleksandra Nova Design takes a black-and-white color palette to new heights, devising elevated, contemporary interiors with plenty of dramatic details. Founder Aleksandra Nova brings a worldly perspective to her designs, which include renovations, new construction, and furnishing projects for residential, commercial, and hospitality clients. Exquisite materials, artful lighting, and sophisticated textiles contribute to the luxurious aesthetic and attention to detail the firm has become known for.

Alisa Bloom brings a world of inspiration to her work, gathering furnishings, textiles, and vintage pieces on her travels across the globe and curating them in her sophisticated interiors. Bloom’s eponymous Chicago and New York-based firm offers interior design and construction management services and specializes in whole-home renovations and new construction. Bloom’s interiors blend innovation and practicality, making them perfect for modern families, and the designer is known for her subtle, mood-creating color palettes. The designer and her team work globally and currently have projects underway in the United States, France, and Hong Kong.

Founded in 2014, Amy Kartheiser Design brings a world of influences into its unique interiors. Founder Amy Kartheiser sources inspiration and unique finds from a variety of far-flung destinations, elevating her clients’ spaces with pieces that reflect their personalities. Balancing sophistication and ease, the firm’s work incorporates and highlights the beauty of a home’s setting through natural materials and soothing color palettes. Kartheiser emphasizes wellness in her designs, creating interiors that enhance her clients’ lives. She’s also the founder of Under the Same Sky, a non-profit that benefits the American Foundation for Suicide Prevention. Kartheiser supports the organization through an online store and pop-up shops featuring her global finds.

For the primary bedroom in a lake house, Amy Storm enhanced the relaxing atmosphere with a neutral palette, soft textures, and natural materials.

Designer Amy Storm earned her stripes in hospitality design before founding her namesake residential firm in 2004. Since then, Storm and her team have become known for their knowledgeable approach and ability to tackle projects of all sizes and styles. The firm works with high-end clientele from across the country who are drawn to her signature “Midwest modernish” style, which blends luxury and livability. Based in Glen Ellyn, Amy Storm & Company is a small but mighty team of interior architects and designers with the experience and expertise to lead projects and keep them running smoothly. The company specializes in custom new construction, incorporating interior design from the start of the project to allow the architecture and interiors to feel completely in sync.

The design brief for this Andrea Goldman project was to create interiors that play nice with blue-chip art.

Natural light and organic materials bring a sense of tranquility to the interiors of Andrea Goldman Design. The Glencoe-based firm guides clients through the entire design process while making sure that even the most complex projects remain on track. From a Florida guest compound designed for a repeat client to a beautifully balanced family home in Winnetka, the firm’s portfolio showcases a wide range of project types and styles, though refinement is a throughline in each design.
